#6977. I Paid a Matchmaker $250,000 to Find My Soulmate. The Result Surprised Me.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago.

Dating apps have become a special kind of hell for singles. People are sick of swiping. They’re tired of the road-to-nowhere conversations and disappointing dates. For all their predictive power, algorithms simply aren’t very good at finding the one.

As a result, people are dumping Tinder left and right. But they haven’t given up on love entirely. In fact, quite the opposite. After years of letting the apps play Cupid, they’re calling in the experts. Google searches for “matchmaker” more than doubled between January 2025 and January 2026. And as interest in matchmaking grows, prices are climbing too. Average fees jumped 26 percent last year. Prices vary enormously, but at the highest end of the market, finding a girlfriend can cost more than a Ferrari.

For the latest installment of “The Secret Lives of Men,” we spoke to Henry*, a 70-year-old man from Atlanta who spent $250,000 on a matchmaker. His fee covered a year of personalized matchmaking, complete with a wardrobe consultant, dating coaches, personality tests, and a nationwide search for eligible women. After eight months and roughly ten introductions, Henry finally met someone he liked.

*Name and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

I’m what I would call a silver fox. I’m 70 years old now, and I’m in excellent physical, mental, and financial shape. I’ve been married twice and dated plenty of women. Over the years, I’ve tried just about every way to meet someone: bars, introductions through friends, dating sites, apps. But at this stage of my life, I can afford the very best—so I decided to shell out $250,000 for a top-of-the-line matchmaker.

Some people spend $250,000 on a car or $75,000 on a vacation in Positano. Others fly private. Sure, you could spend a fraction of that money, but you’d get a lesser experience. I look at matchmaking the same way. Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago. I’m interested in finding someone I’m actually compatible with.

I’ve actually used cheaper matchmakers before, the kind who charge around $25,000 for a few months. Mostly they just save you the trouble of going through dating apps yourself. They’ll find a few women on Tinder, a few more somewhere else, and send you their profiles. This time I wanted someone who would actually get to know me and put some thought into whom she set me up with. That’s why I hired Sameera Sullivan. She works with only three or four clients at a time and charges $250,000 for a year of services that go far beyond what the other matchmakers offered. Those guys were like a Lexus. Sameera is a Bugatti.

The first thing she did was bring in a few professionals to help with my dating profile. A wardrobe consultant went through my clothes and suggested a few changes. A photographer took my pictures, and a professional writer put together my bio. Then I had several sessions with a dating coach and took a bunch of personality tests to figure out what kind of woman I’d be most compatible with.

Each date I went on helped Sameera narrow things down further. After every introduction, she’d ask both me and the woman she’d set me up with how it went. Even if we stopped seeing each other after two or three dates, she’d try to figure out why and use what she learned to make a better match next time. It’s kind of like AI. The more information she had, the better the results. The tenth woman she introduced me to ended up being the one. Sameera actually found her on LinkedIn and reached out to see if she’d be interested in going out with me.

We’ve been dating for almost three months now, and things are going extremely well. Everyone says we have big couples energy. She’s in her mid-50s, which is good because I look and act younger than my biological age. Our love languages match, our personalities complement each other, and I feel more compatible with her than I did with either of my former wives.

One thing I really like about her is that she’s got a bit of an adventurous streak. I’m naturally pretty steady and set in my ways, but I have an adventurous side too, and she brings it out in me. Like the other night, we were having dinner at a Mexican restaurant, and someone mentioned there was a speakeasy downstairs. Normally I would’ve said we should try it another time. But I saw her face light up, so I said, “Okay, let’s go.” We went in and had a drink. I probably wouldn’t have done that on my own.

I’ve put my matchmaking contract on pause for now. If this relationship doesn’t work out, I still have about four months of service left. For now, I’m very happy with how things are going. The funniest thing about it all, though, is that the woman Sameera found for me, the one she searched all over the country for—she actually lives in my neighborhood.

#6973. Dialed In: All the Time in the World

Accutron’s bold new Horizon Chronograph unites all the best elevated complications in watchmaking in a single watch at a fantastic price.

Every now and then, a watch comes along that demolishes all your preconceived notions of the way things are in watchmaking. This week, Accutron released the Horizon, an entirely new timepiece (in two colorways, blue and black) that positively bristles with complications normally associated with five- and six-figure watches and at a very attractive price to boot.

It's a bold move from the brand that created the very first fully electronic watch back in 1960. With that model, Accutron debuted its tuning fork technology, by which a traditional mechanical watch escapement, with all its susceptibility to friction and gravity, was replaced with a tiny metal tuning fork powered by electricity via a built-in transistor. What made the tuning fork ground-breaking was the fact that, under power, it vibrated at a constant and continuous rate. And it made the aptly named Accutron (hence the name) accurate to plus or minus one second a month, an unimagined degree of accuracy at that point, and, frankly, for a long time after too.

Back to the Horizon. First up in a litany of nifty and rather mind-blowing gizmos is the fact that this Master Complication tells you the time not only with the hands of the watch in the conventional way, but also with sound. This watch is a minute repeater, chiming the current time in hours, then quarter hours, then minutes at the press of a button. Derived in the 18th century from the need to know the time at night a good century before electric light and SuperLuminova markers made their appearance, the minute repeater could—once you got the hang of it—tell you the exact time even in pitch darkness. But it's a complication so rare and expensive to make, that we honestly can't think of a minute repeater ever produced at such a compelling price.

The bells and whistles don't stop there. With three sub dials in glittery black sandstone that looks like the night sky) set into a granular dial made from Denchu, another granular sandstone, all beneath the conventional center hands, this watch is both a Perpetual Calendar and Chronograph. It features the day and the date, the leap year, the phases of the moon, a second time zone and a day/night indicator, all packed into an elegant and sporty 41.5mm stainless steel case. This, by the way, is made in a superior 904L steel with a matching steel bracelet, an alloy known for its deep luster when polished, as well as its resistance to corrosion and pitting. Underlying all this style is the Horizon's substance; the watch is driven by a quartz movement that offers excellent accuracy.

If you want to understand the meaning of time, you only have to gaze into the Horizon's dial, which breaks it all down for you in a very cool package. Reading it will keep you busy for days.

Jaden Smith Is Taking Louboutin into the Future

#6967. Netflix Is Stepping Onto the Ice With an Icebreaker Adaptation—Here’s Everything We Know

Hockey romances aren’t just dominating bookshelves anymore; they’ve become television’s newest obsession.

Following the breakout success of recent sports romances like Heated Rivalry and Off Campus, Netflix is officially joining the trend with a series adaptation of Hannah Grace’s bestselling novel Icebreaker. The BookTok sensation, which helped introduce a new generation of readers to the hockey romance genre, is headed to the streamer as a college-set drama centered on a figure skater and a hockey player whose ambitions—and attraction—begin to collide.

Here’s everything we know about Icebreaker so far.

Yes. Netflix announced on June 15 that it has greenlit a television adaptation of Hannah Grace’s novel Icebreaker.

The project was unveiled during the Banff World Media Festival by Jinny Howe, Netflix’s head of scripted series for the U.S. and Canada.

In a statement, Howe said, “Netflix has a long history of turning beloved romance novels into massive global hits. We know our members can’t get enough of these stories, which makes Icebreaker the perfect next chapter for us.”

She added, “By supercharging the hockey sports romance genre, Icebreaker has ignited a passionate fandom and helped redefine the landscape of contemporary romance. We are thrilled to partner with Jade Bartlett, Amanda Lasher, and the team at Unwell Productions to bring this irresistible love story to life.”

The series follows Anastasia Allen, a competitive figure skater focused on reaching the Olympics, and Nate Hawkins, a star hockey player with dreams of going professional.

Their paths cross when an accident forces their teams to share the same rink, creating tension both on and off the ice.

Netflix’s official synopsis reads, “She’s the transfer gunning for Olympic gold. He’s the campus star chasing the NHL. But when an accident forces their teams to share one rink, Anastasia Allen and Nate Hawkins collide—and discipline gives way to obsession, because the only thing more dangerous than wanting to win is wanting each other.”

The adaptation will be set in the same college sports world that readers first fell in love with in Grace’s novel, blending romance, competition, and the pressures of elite athletics.

Yes. The show is based on Grace’s 2022 novel of the same name.

As the first book in Grace’s Maple Hills series, the novel’s popularity eventually expanded into a larger franchise, with follow-up books Wildfire and Daydream taking place in the same university setting.

Amanda Lasher has signed on as showrunner, writer, and executive producer. Her previous television credits include Gossip Girl (2008–2012), Riverdale (2017–2018), and The Bold Type (2018–2019).

Jade Bartlett developed the adaptation and wrote the pilot. She will also write and executive produce the series alongside Lasher. Bartlett’s previous projects include Road House, Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, and Miller’s Girl.

“I love this genre, and it’s been a joy working with Jade, the team at Unwell, and Netflix to bring Hannah Grace’s beloved Icebreaker novel to the screen,” Lasher said in a statement.

Bartlett added, “It’s been such a joy to play in Hannah Grace’s world of Icebreaker, a story filled with ambitious, talented, and undeniably sexy characters. But the real special sauce is that, above all, they are good, deeply compassionate people.”

The series will be executive produced by Unwell Productions, with Alex Cooper, Matt Kaplan, and Mina Lefevre serving as executive producers.

On September 28, Deadline revealed the show’s two leads. Daisy Kelley and Connor Simos will star as Anastasia “Stassie” Allen and Nate Hawkins, respectively.

On September 29, Gabriel Tierney (Rivals, Enola Holmes 2), Auliʻi Cravalho (Moana), Peter Berry, Chaneil Kular (Sex Education), Devin Craig (Sex Lives of College Girls), and Aidan Shaw (We Were Liars, Heated Rivalry) were also announced as cast members on the show. According to Deadline, “Tierney will play Aaron; Cravalho will portray Lola; Berry is Robbie; Kular will play JJ; Craig will portray Henry; and Shaw is Russ.”

On November 11, Netflix announced that Paul Wesley (The Vampire Diaries) will also be joining the cast of Icebreaker. He’ll star as Coach Neil Faulkner in the series.

The series does not currently have a release date, but the show is currently in production in Vancouver, Canada.

#6961. The 6 Smartphones to Buy in 2026

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We don’t care if you’re on team Apple or Android. We’ve found you the phone to upgrade to.

You’ve landed here, so let me guess: You’re an iPhone person and you want a new iPhone. Or ... you’re an Android person, dedicated to your brand of choice already, be it Google or Samsung. Here’s the thing: Your choice of phone says a lot about you, but realistically you’ve already locked yourself into choosing between the same three phones all your friends are getting. We are humans, so our brains are finely tuned to love habits. The result is not too many people want to adjust to a new way of interfacing with daily life every other year; they just want the same thing. There’s a chance you are the rare third kind of person who wants a change. In all three cases, there’s good news. You kinda already know what you want (and don’t want) out of a smartphone.

My fellow editors and I have tried the latest in what Apple, Samsung, and Google have to offer, and have determined for ourselves which are the greatest. This year, the biggest shake-up to the race is foldable phones. Not entirely a new category, but the iPhone Duo is the capstone on a past 12 months that have legitimized the form across both Apple and Android devices. You’ll be paying a premium, and not all the software is properly optimized yet, but the tech is finally there. For everyone else, there’s still the new pro model iPhone or the latest A series Pixel for the value hunters. Regardless of whether you’re after a bold reinvention, or just an improved camera and a slightly shinier display, you’ll find something to love in these six smartphones.

I’ve made vocal arguments in favor of going against the grain and getting a Windows device instead of a MacBook, but at the end of the day it’s all too easy to stick with it if you’re on team iPhone already. The 18 Pro is the iPhone to get if you’re a daily power user, on your phone 24/7 for both work and personal reasons. It’s got a glorious 120-hertz-refresh-rate retina screen, exactly the smoothness and crispness you’ve come to expect from a pro level phone.

It’s not merely the one we’ve seen the most in the wild, it’s the best bet for an iPhone that will last you another four years. And while we can't say we love the new design, it’s been easy to get used to. And functionally, it’s the iPhone Pro we know and love. It is fast, it’s got a smooth 120 hertz screen and it has one of the best cameras you can get on a phone. If there’s one upgrade we truly feel each time we get a new phone, it’s the camera. This year’s is no exception.

Compared with the other Android offerings out there, the Pixel 11 Pro is in elite territory. It’s not technically as beefy as a Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra or ZFold 8 Ultra, but it’s notably a lot cheaper than either of those phones.

It’s fast and it’s got that smooth refresh rate a high-end phone should. Best of all, holy shit is the camera something else? It can take one incredible photo after another and it’s probably equipped with the best selfie camera on a phone. Our editors who’ve tested this one also won’t shut up about how much better Gemini is than every other AI assistant. Finally, a “smart” feature that lives up to the moniker. And compared to the equivalent iPhones and Samsung phones, the battery life reigns supreme—even if, as a trade-off, it takes the longest to charge.

If you’re of the Android persuasion, it’s easy to see the iPhone 18 Pro as the other team’s Google Pixel 11 Pro, instead of the other way around. On a pure specs level it’s a better phone—just probably not better enough to get Apple diehards over the OS gap. Serious sarcasm aside, it’s not the iPhone users who need this phone. It’s everyone currently suffering on an older Pixel model or, worse, some no-name Android phone.

It was only a couple of years ago you could read me advising you not to buy a renewed iPhone. But with how expensive all technology has become recently, there is now a wide enough gulf between something like this and a new iPhone to make buying refurbished worth the cost savings. Instead of buying a 17e new for $700, pick up this refurbished 16e—it’s half the price, and you won’t be living too far in the past.

The 16e is pretty much everything a phone should be and nothing extraneous. This phone is light, yet durable. It’s got the Action Button from the 16 lineup but does lack the Camera Control.

The other omission of note is the lack of MagSafe functionality. The 16e supports wireless Qi charging, but it does not magnetize the way every iPhone since the 12 (barring the SE line) has. Once again, we don’t think this matters to the core audience for this phone, so it’s less of a flaw and more of a way Apple has honed in on exactly the audience it wants to hit for this one.

For $600, this is the most affordable big-name smartphone. There are plenty of Androids in the $200 to $300 range, but by and large they aren’t meant to last. They are poorly made stopgaps to get you to something like the Pixel 10a. Right now for only $400, this is the best value you can get on a brand-new smartphone.

It has a lot of what we love from the newest Pixel phones—including the latest OS features like Gemini and the invaluable Call Screening feature that automatically detects spam. And while the front-facing camera isn’t mind-blowing compared with the 10 series Pixels, it does still rock a very impressive wide-angle back camera that offers more variation than the e series of iPhones.

The iPhone Duo is the most innovative and groundbreaking phone release of the year. We said as much in our gadget awards and editor Luke Gullory has been giving his updates along the way. He’s tested and tested, and the verdict is in. This one is a keeper and, most importantly, it’s the best foldable phone to hit the market yet.

The genius touch here is the aspect ratio. For the Duo, Apple went with a √2:1 (or 1.414:1) aspect ratio. That is slightly different from the other foldable, but the subtle change makes a difference. On the Duo, the small outside screen exactly mirrors the size of half the large inner screen, but it also exactly mirrors the total aspect ratio of the entire large inner screen. The matte screen seems odd at first, but it’s the solution Apple has found to showing any creasing and, once again, this gives it that design edge. There’s a reason people start to care when Apple does it.

I’ll also note it has the same chipset and specs as the 18 Pro, including RAM. Normally, this would put it on par power-wise, but the real-world performance lags behind due to the amount of thermal throttling needed to pull off the design miracle of the form factor.

If you want a flip phone, you can shop vintage for pennies but when it comes to using those like daily phones, the truth is they don’t support modern cellular networks. They are as good as a toy. But if you still want that flip phone experience, Samsung’s most affordable new folding phone gives you something of the sort.

Don’t get me wrong, the Z Flip8 is still expensive, starting at $1,200. But compared to all the other foldables from Samsung and Google, it is technically the least expensive one. That’s largely because it’s got the least amount of screen space. Really it’s just a normal-sized Galaxy phone with a front-screen, not something that has a unique aspect ratio like the Duo or the Galaxy Z Fold 8 phones. On a specs level, it’s on par with these phones. Crisp 120 hertz screen, solid front and rear cameras, and all the Android app support you’d want. Really, though, if you’re committing it’s a commitment to having a wacky, folding flip phone that you can set up like a tent or show off all the different camera modes.

Sunscreen is always important, but when one product can also brighten your complexion and take the place of your favorite concealer, it becomes impossible to forget. And when it’s made specifically for the eye area, it can make one of the most delicate parts of your skin care routine feel a whole lot easier.

“Tinted sunscreens are one of those rare beauty products where the cosmetic benefit and the dermatological benefit really align,” says Dr. Pearl Ugwu-Dike, a board-certified dermatologist. “You’re protecting your skin from tomorrow’s sun damage while making it look good today.” As for why they’re especially useful under the eyes, the skin in that area is some of the most delicate that we have on our body. “That makes it particularly susceptible to visible signs of photoaging, like fine lines, crepiness, hyperpigmentation,” Dr. Ugwu-Dike says. “It’s also often a place that gets neglected when applying sunscreen, conventionally.” That’s why the best tinted SPFs for under-eyes are so special: They offer protection while providing immediate color correction and a brighter-looking appearance, helping to camouflage under-eye bags and dark circles.

Of course, the right formula depends on how much coverage you want, your skin type, and how your under-eye area tends to behave throughout the day. #6952. How Does Diplo Survive a Weekend Running, DJing, and Partying? His Whoop Data Tells the Story.

The 47-year-old DJ shared his health stats from a frenzied weekend that could’ve killed any normal person. Here’s how he does it without coming apart at the seams.

On a Saturday morning in August, at exactly 10:00 a.m., August Thomas Wesley Pentz, the DJ known as Diplo, stood outside the Barlume Beach Club in Montauk, Long Island, and delivered a sermon to hundreds of runners. “I’m very tired; I slept three hours for you guys,” he shouted through a megaphone, dressed in gray cargo shorts, a tank top, and pink aviator shades. “But now we’re ready to run. Let’s have a good time.”

Then one of the world’s most prolific DJs launched himself into a 5K through the sand and rocks of Gin Beach. It was a run-club event organized by A Team, a dating app for the wellness obsessed, and Diplo’s Run Club, which stages nine group runs across the U.S. (and, soon, Europe).

As I watched him that sunny and hot morning, I couldn’t stop thinking: How the hell is this guy still standing, let alone leading a run? It was only Saturday, and Diplo was already deep into a frenzied weekend. He landed in New York City on Thursday morning after taking the overnight flight from his home in Malibu. That evening he DJed from 12:30 a.m. to 3:00 a.m. at the Edge, a venue 100 floors above the city. On Friday, he boarded a seaplane for the Hamptons, where he performed two more sets: a sunset show at Surf Lodge, where Jimmy Buffett and Snoop Dogg have played to crowds of models and billionaires, followed by an impromptu set at Mary Lou’s, a Montauk club on the harbor where he only meant to hang out but ended up taking the decks. He didn’t leave until around 1:00 a.m.

I was at both venues and felt deeply, existentially hungover the next morning. I figured—hoped, really—the run would include some walking. Diplo, however, led the pack. He wore a Whoop, the health tracker with which he has a partnership, and reached a heart rate of 167 beats per minute. “He wasn’t taking it easy at all,” said Greg Grosicki, a staff research scientist at Whoop who reviewed Diplo’s data from his weekend in Montauk.

I didn’t understand how a 47-year-old pulled off this feat—the travel, the DJ sets, the cavorting, the running—without collapsing and retreating to his hotel suite for the rest of the weekend. On Saturday evening he had two more sets, one at the annual gala of the Surfrider Foundation, which supports ocean conservation and includes Sean McPherson and John Slattery as supporters. After that he DJed a private party for investment executive Michael Dorrell before flying private to Ibiza overnight for his next gig.

After the run—which, miraculously, I did finish—I talked with Diplo about how he survives this lifestyle. He even shared his Whoop data with me from the weekend so I could compare his body’s reaction with how he told me he felt. “I’m actually fully cracked out every day, doing way more than running and DJing, more than anybody else,” he said.

He likes to sound nonchalant about it. “When I did an interview with some of the Whoop counselors, they were like, your body is very similar mentally to a Navy SEAL or an ER nurse. You’re able to just get up and go do whatever,” he said. “I can always get up and get shit done, unless I’m sick.” Okay, so maybe Diplo was just born with it. But I learned that he does more for his physical and mental health than he likes to let on. This is the story of how a 47-year-old, internationally renowned DJ, who works and parties into the predawn hours, travels the world, and leads packs of people on runs, pulls it off without coming apart at the seams.

Running appeals to him because it travels well. All you need is a pair of shoes and the will to put them on. “I do things I can do anywhere,” he said. “Running is free.” He aims for 15 miles a week across three or four runs, averaging a seven-and-a-half to eight-minute mile. His fastest mile, six and a half minutes, came after his run club appeared on the prediction platform Polymarket, where people bet against him. “That’s what motivated me to run pretty fast,” he said with a laugh.

DJing counts as cardio too. After all, his sets typically run at least two hours. “It’s a mix of dancing and standing,” he said. “It’s almost like a little air squat.” He’s baffled that his dancers, several from the group Major Lazer, stay in shape without a gym. “I’m like, ‘What do you guys do?’ ” he said. “They’re like, ‘We don’t do anything. We just dance at your shows.’ ”

The numbers back him up: During his Surf Lodge set, his max heart rate hit 117 bpm. “If you consider these sets are two hours long, maintaining a heart rate of 120 is a real physical activity,” Greg Grosicki, the research scientist at Whoop, told me. “It’s equivalent to riding a bike for two hours. You feel pretty wrecked after that, and he’s getting equivalent strain from his sets.”

Diplo mixes cardio and weights in HIIT sessions and does 100 to 200 push-ups a day, several times a week, usually in his hotel room. “It takes five minutes of your life,” he said.

Once a week he wears an electrical muscle stimulation, or EMS, suit for 20 minutes. “It does pulsing with simple moves,” he said, naming a brand called Catalyst and a newer one, Pepper, he’s about to try. He credits the suit, half jokingly, for his arm muscles.

He also takes hot Pilates classes with light weights and works out at Barry’s Bootcamp near his home in Malibu. “There’s an unwritten rule about group fitness in California: You don’t bother celebrities,” he said. “You let them get their job done, then you tell people you saw Chris Martin. Every time I go to Pilates, he’s next to me.”

According to the data, long flights, especially red-eyes, hit Diplo hard, said Grosicki. After an overnight flight to Ibiza, Diplo’s recovery score—a measure of how ready the body is to perform—dropped to 21 percent, and his sleep score to 29 percent. That’s well below average for him. “He’s human, and he’s responding to these stressors,” Grosicki said.

“I do like to get the red-eyes, which probably messes up my sleep the most, but I’d rather be outside in the day,” Diplo said. “Being on a plane during the day is so depressing.” He said he struggles to hold a circadian rhythm and sometimes crashes in the midafternoon. On Thursday, for example, he slept five hours in the middle of the day ahead of his set at the Edge.

Winding down after a set is its own challenge. “I might take a Xanax if I have to,” he said. “I got to stop thinking about things.” He also puts his phone away, listens to audiobooks and history podcasts, skips food before bed (though he does chug electrolytes), and uses PEMF mats, which send low-frequency electromagnetic waves through the body to aid in relaxation. Mostly, though, he just takes what sleep he can get.

Saturday morning, after two Friday-night sets in Montauk, his recovery score was 67 percent and his sleep score 65 percent, both above average. On Monday, after he spent the night in Ibiza, his recovery score was 56 percent and sleep score was 62 percent. As Grosicki put it, “He bounces back.”

Diplo avoids drinking before shows and on travel days, and he hasn’t done recreational drugs in six months. When he does, it’s acid. “I would run on acid, just try things on acid,” he said. “I could fly a plane on acid.”

“Mental health is pretty much everything,” Diplo said. “But you have to get there sometimes by exercising. Finding your body, being in tune with your body, is probably the first thing you can do to have a clear mind, to get rid of any sickness you have, whatever’s keeping you down. That’s the first way to start.”

He’s tried more unconventional resets too. Seven years ago, he smoked toad venom. “I’ll never do it again, but it was pretty hardcore,” he said. “It’s a really big ego death. Being humbled is very good wellness.”

Whatever he’s doing, it seems to be working, at least by the numbers. Whether the rest of us should try it is a different question entirely.

#6948. Sales Development Representative (East Coast) at LiveKit

LiveKit is the default infrastructure layer for real-time AI, the platform that gives AI the ability to see, hear, and speak. From powering voice for millions of ChatGPT users to enabling drone teleoperation at Skydio and serving as the backbone for roughly 25% of 911 emergency calls in the U.S., LiveKit handles the real-time media layer that production AI applications depend on.

Over 300,000 developers and 5,000+ companies from leading AI labs to Fortune 500 enterprises run production workloads on LiveKit. We've raised $181M from investors including Index Ventures, Altimeter Capital, and Salesforce Ventures, and we're growing fast. Now we're building the sales team to match.

We're hiring an Enterprise SDR to drive top-of-funnel pipeline for our enterprise sales motion. You'll be the first impression LiveKit makes on engineering leaders, product organizations, and AI/ML teams at companies building the next generation of voice and multimodal AI applications.

This is not a boilerplate SDR role. Our buyers are technical staff engineers, heads of infrastructure, VP-level AI product leaders and they can smell a templated sequence from a mile away. You'll need to understand what LiveKit actually does, why developers reach for it over rolling their own WebRTC stack, and how to translate that into compelling, relevant outreach for each account you work.

You'll partner closely with our enterprise AEs and work directly with sales and marketing leadership tto sharpen messaging and positioning as we scale our GTM functions.

Own outbound pipeline generation targeting Fortune 2000 accounts across key verticals: including healthcare, financial services, and technology companies.

Partner tightly with enterprise AEs on account strategy you'll co-own named accounts, align on entry points, and run coordinated multi-threaded plays to break into large organizations

Research target accounts to craft highly personalized, technically credible outreach via email, LinkedIn, and phone

Book discovery calls and product demos for enterprise AEs; provide full context so they walk in sharp and prepared

Work closely with the broader GTM org marketing, solutions engineering, and product on campaign strategy, ICP refinement, and feedback loops that sharpen our enterprise motion

Surface market intelligence back to the product and GTM teams as you're often the first to hear what buyers care about

Hit and exceed monthly and quarterly pipeline targets

3–5 years of SDR or BDR experience, ideally at a developer tools, infrastructure, or voice AI company

Genuine curiosity about technology; you don't need to write code, but you should be able to hold an intelligent conversation about why a company might choose a managed WebRTC platform over a self-hosted stack

Strong written communication - you know the difference between an email that gets deleted and one that gets a reply

Experience with sales engagement tools and Salesforce

Track record of consistently meeting or exceeding quota

Comfort working in a fast-moving, early-stage environment where the playbook is still being written and flexibility is required

Background selling to engineering, DevOps, or AI/ML teams

Familiarity with voice AI, real-time communications, or WebRTC concepts

Experience at an open-source company with a PLG motion - you understand what it means when a developer has been building with your product for 6 months before the first sales conversation

Experience using AI tools to accelerate prospecting and personalization at scale

Enormous market. Real-time AI is becoming a requirement for nearly every enterprise application. We're the infrastructure layer, and we're well ahead of the field.

Incredible developer traction. Our sales team doesn't cold-call strangers they engage with a community that already loves the product.

Technical credibility. You'll sell something that engineers respect. That matters if you care about representing products you believe in.

Trajectory. This is a ground-floor opportunity on an enterprise sales team at a company with serious investor backing, strong revenue growth, and the reference customers to close big deals.

Competitive comp: Base + uncapped commission + equity. We want SDRs who want to win.

#6943. The Saga of Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s Fake Leather Jacket

The Internet loved his outrageous new look. There was just one problem.

There are several ways to respond when you’ve admitted to an affair, your company’s shares are tumbling, and even the White House is discussing your private life. Turning up in a spectacularly studded Saint Laurent leather jacket is one of them. Resigning is another.

For a while, it seemed as if Jensen Huang, the 63-year-old former CEO of Nvidia, had opted for the former. A photograph showing the tech billionaire in an archival Hedi Slimane YSL biker jacket went viral this week. The Internet was delighted. There was just one problem: The photograph was fake.

The resignation, which came just this morning, was real.

As CNN reported, the image had been doctored from a genuine photograph taken at a Seoul airport in June. Nvidia, which one might imagine has other things to deal with right now, confirmed it wasn’t real.

But perhaps the reason so many people believed it was that Huang already dresses like this. Leather jackets are his uniform. He reportedly owns 30 or 40 of them, including a snazzy reptile-effect number worn to deliver his company’s keynote at the Consumer Electronics Show.

And Hedi Slimane was the perfect designer to bring into the fantasy. The Frenchman, who ran Saint Laurent from 2012 to 2016, made his name dressing skinny rock stars and turning their disheveled, devil-may-care attitude—later rebranded as so-called “indie sleaze”—into expensive luxury fashion. His leather jackets were practically a uniform for men who were determined to look as though they didn’t care what anyone thought and/or having a midlife crisis.

For a billionaire in the middle of a spectacularly public scandal, it was quite a look. One Instagram commenter described Huang as the first person to achieve “negative aura” in archival Hedi.

There’s another irony. Huang has previously said his wife, Lori—i.e., the wronged party in all this—helps choose his clothes.

And then there’s the technology itself. Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution, including the tools capable of ... producing convincing fake photographs.

In conclusion, then, the image wasn’t real. But as a portrait of a billionaire in the middle of a spectacularly public crisis, we so wanted it to be.

#6941. The 17 Best Comedy Movies of 2026

Thanks to The Invite, Coyote vs. Acme, and Stop! That! Train!, big-screen comedy is making a comeback.

Are comedies making a comeback? Over the past decade, you couldn’t get a laugh into theaters even if it was forced. Hollywood decided that comedies weren’t worth the same treatment as superhero tentpoles or low-budget horror, and just about every legacy franchise sequel of a former ‘90s comedy disappointed audiences because they were basically cash grabs that played on nostalgia. Comedy needed help.

So, in 2026, some fresh films forced Hollywood to come to its senses. Coyote vs. Acme, a Looney Tunes film that Warner Bros. originally dropped to claim a tax write-off, grossed over $126 million at the box office. Olivia Wilde’s The Invite might just receive nominations for every major award category at the Oscars, and Airplane!-esque humor made a comeback in RuPaul’s hilarious disaster flick Stop! That! Train!

If you’re in need of a belly laugh, check out the best comedies of 2026 below.

Don’t get me wrong, Coyote vs. Acme is no Space Jam. But the Looney Tunes film that triumphantly won the box office—even after Warner Bros. gave up on it—is still one of the funniest movies of the year. The story follows Wile E. Coyote as he partners with a lowly injury attorney (Will Forte) to sue the ACME Corporation for its defective products. The comedy also stars John Cena as ACME’s defense attorney, who probably has the funniest line of the year when he tells Tweety Bird: “I don’t pay you to tink you taw, I pay you to taw.”

Olivia Colman stars in this romantic comedy drama about a lonely fisherwoman who grows tired of the town’s harassment due to her smell. So, she asks a magical basket weaver (Peter Dinklage) to make her a husband (Alexander Skarsgård) out of wicker. What follows is a bizarre yet hilarious story about the follies of marriage, jealousy, and tons of sex with a man made out of basket material.

Sam Rockwell leads this sci-fi comedy by former Pirates of the Caribbean director Gore Verbinski, about a time-traveler who travels to the same diner over and over again in a Groundhog Day–esque loop because he believes that if he can take the right configuration of people from the diner back to the future with him then he may be able to save the world from AI’s takeover. I know, it’s strange. But Verbinski makes the comedy work, especially when you learn about each diner patron via small vignettes emphasizing various doomsday scenarios.

Real estate agent Rudd Landy (John Cena) is called to the hospital when his brother gets into a car accident. Turns out, the man in emergency care is not his real brother (Christopher Meloni), but someone that he used to mentor when he was younger as part of a Big Brothers Big Sisters program. Without anywhere else to go, Rudd agrees to help take care of his “little brother” (Eric André) while he recovers, even if his little brother might just ruin his life.

When Gail Daughtry’s (Zoey Deutch) husband takes their celebrity sex pass joke literally, Gail sets out to get even by tracking down her celebrity sex pass—John Hamm—to have sex with him and even out the score. The comedy film also stars “Weird Al” Yankovic, Ben Wang, Sabrina Impacciatore, Joe Lo Truglio, Fred Melamed, and Mad Men’s John Slattery as himself.

Johnny Knoxville, Steve-O, and the whole Jackass crew return for one last ride in Jackass: Best and Last, another compilation of skits, stunts, and pranks from some of the most fearless people on the planet. They hit each other in the groin with wooden mallets, hook their penises up to shock collars, and reminisce about some of their most iconic sketches throughout the years.

Half RuPaul’s Drag Race, half Airplane! for Bravo fans, Stop! That! Train! is the dumbest movie I’ve seen all year (complimentary). The film follows RuPaul and her Drag Race stars as they attempt to stop a speeding bullet train heading straight toward the storm of the century, aka a Stormaganza. Even if you’re so out of your depth that you have no idea what’s going on, Stop! That! Train! argues that that’s okay. It’s comedy for comedy’s sake, and we need more of that at the theater.

Mel Brooks just turned 100 years old (!!!). But before that, he was 99 in Mel Brooks: The 99-Year-Old Man!—a two-part HBO documentary directed by Judd Apatow. The film, which chronicles the comedian’s career throughout The Producers, Blazing Saddles, Spaceballs, Young Frankenstein, and many more, also touches on his work as a film producer. Still, it’s the testimonies from his friendship with Carl Reiner and his marriage to Anne Bancroft that really pull at the heartstrings after a good laugh down memory lane.

Olivia Wilde is not afraid of Virginia Woolf. In the actress’s latest film, The Invite, the classic dinner-party setup full of playful talking and relationship arguments somehow morphs into a sex comedy. The whole film takes place in Angela (Wilde) and Joe’s (Seth Rogen) apartment as they welcome the raucous upstairs neighbors Pina (Penelope Cruz) and Hawk (Edward Norton). But unlike Who’s Afraid of Virginia Woolf? and its descent into anger and grief, The Invite asks more questions about love, commitment, and everyone’s stance on orgies.

The struggling music duo always works for me. Whether it’s Flight of the Conchords or Ishtar, there’s something funny about people with no talent still trying to make it to the top. Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie takes the genre to maybe its lowest budget yet, as the Canadian web-series comedians somehow also make room to spoof Back to the Future and incorporate real Nathan Fielder–like guerrilla filmmaking.

One of the coolest movies to premiere out of SXSW was the Hulu original Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice, from writer/director BenDavid Grabinski. A loan shark (Vince Vaughn) uses a time machine to undo one bad night involving his close friend (James Marsden) and his wife (Eiza González). Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice is the kind of movie you want when the pizza is hot, the beers are cold, and your friends are indecisive about what you all want to watch.

After years of creative and financial slump, Pixar has found its groove again with Hoppers. Essentially Animal Farm meets Avatar, Hoppers tells the story of a genius college student whose participation in a breakthrough science experiment lets her become a beaver who can communicate with animals. Though her efforts to help animals seem promising at first, she quickly realizes what really happens when animals (and bugs!) fight back against mankind. Directed by We Bare Bears creator Daniel Chong, Hoppers is an easygoing, family-friendly romp through the wilderness.

Liam Neeson continues his funny streak in the action comedy Cold Storage. Joe Keery (Stranger Things) plays a slacker employee of a storage facility who, along with his cute coworker (Georgina Campbell), discovers a deadly, mutating fungus. Teaming up with a grizzled federal agent (Neeson), the three work to save the world. Basically a modern-day Outbreak, Cold Storage isn’t one to leave dormant on your watch list.

Dave Bautista and Jason Momoa make up the ultimate action-movie tag team in The Wrecking Crew. The guys play estranged brothers in Hawaii who work together to piece together who killed their father and how on earth the Japanese yakuza is involved.

Boots Riley, the writer and director of Sorry to Bother You, does it again with his crime caper I Love Boosters. In this anticapitalist satire, a crew of professional thieves works to take down a ruthless fashion giant (Demi Moore). Keke Palmer, Naomi Ackie, Taylour Paige, Poppy Liu, Eiza González, and more make up this delirious ensemble that takes Ocean’s Eleven and dresses it in hyper-surreal shades.

Sometimes we need a nauseatingly cute rom-com. Kevin James (yes, The King of Queens) stars in this breezy and uplifting comedy about a man who, after being left at the altar, goes on his Italian honeymoon solo. While there, he makes new friends and even deeper connections with strangers halfway around the world. A feel-good flick about new beginnings, Solo Mio can lift you high when you’re feeling down.

A stoner comedy? In 2026? In this economy? Well, the streaming generation can finally know what it’s like to have their own Harold & Kumar with Pizza Movie. Gaten Matarazzo and Sean Giambrone star as college roommates and social pariahs who, while high on a new hallucinogenic drug, embark on a psychedelic odyssey to make it downstairs and get their pizza delivery. Despite a popular meme mocking the movie’s marketing, Pizza Movie performed well with critics at its SXSW premiere.

‘You Can See Everything’ Is Worth the Hype

MobLand Episode 8 Takes a Devastating Turn

The 18 Best Netflix Shows of 2026 (So Far)

#6939. A You’ve Got Mail Reboot Is in the Works—Here’s What We Know So Far

Clueless, 13 Going on 30, The Families Stone, and now, You’ve Got Mail—Nora Ephron’s 1998 classic is the latest beloved title to receive a reboot for the modern era. Will the film’s defining emails be exchanged for Instagram and TikTok DMs? And better yet, will Meg Ryan and Tom Hanks be involved in the 2020s version of the film? Plot details are incredibly sparse, but here’s what we know about the You’ve Got Mail reboot so far.

Details are currently under wraps, but it will likely not follow the original movie’s plot exactly. “Rather than a remake of the original 1998 film starring Tom Hanks and Meg Ryan, the new film will follow a new story,” The Hollywood Reporter revealed.

The original film, based on Miklós László’s 1937 Hungarian play Parfumerie, follows independent bookstore owner Kathleen Kelly (Ryan) and a corporate bookshop magnate Joe Fox (Hanks) as they fall in love anonymously through the internet, but they have no knowledge that he’s trying to put her out of business.

Jason Orley (Close Personal Friends, Bupkis, and I Want You Back) is directing and writing the current draft of the project, based on a screenplay from Deborah Kaplan and Harry Elfont. The original film’s producers, Neal Moritz and Tania Landau, are returning for the reboot.

Ryan and Hanks aren’t currently attached to the You’ve Got Mail reboot, but most details of the movie are not public yet. (Fingers crossed.)

The studio has not shared any details on which stars will be featured in the project. Fans will have to stay tuned for further details.

#6936. I Can Feel the Tremors of History Rumbling Beneath Scott Colom’s Victory

The senator-elect from Mississippi invoked Medger Evers in his winning speech, which reminded me of how far we’ve come—and how far we still have to go.

(Optional Musical Accompaniment to This Post)

Okay! Now we can celebrate. From the Mississippi Free Press:

Oh, to hell with the numbers. Let’s just celebrate a win by a guy cool enough to receive the good news of his election while wearing a Richard Pryor hoodie. From his victory speech:

One day in 1994, in the employ of another magazine, I went down to Jackson on assignment for a story about the death penalty that ultimately never ran. It was the day after the Republicans, led by Representative Newt Gingrich, won control of the entire Congress for the first time since 1952 and control of the House of Representatives for the first time since 1954. It can be argued, and I have, that these results scared the courage out of the Democratic party as thoroughly as George McGovern’s landslide loss to Richard Nixon in 1972 did. And when the modern Democratic party gets scared, it inevitably runs away from its most sacred commitments and the proudest moments in its history. This result caused Democratic president Bill Clinton to sign grotesque legislation like the Defense of Marriage Act and a crime bill that, relevant to my mission to Mississippi, added 28 new death-penalty crimes to the federal code. The bar in the Walthall Hotel, where I was staying, was filled every night by gloating Republicans, which drove me to room service for the balance of my stay.

On my last day in Jackson, I took a drive across town to what was then called 2332 Guynes Street in the Elraine subdivision, which had been developed in the 1950s by men named Winston Thompson and Leroy Burnett in order to service the rising Black middle class of the city. One of the houses in the development was purchased by Medgar and Myrlie Evers. On the night of June 12, 1963, Myrlie and their children were watching the speech in which President John F. Kennedy finally got off the dime on civil rights when they heard Medgar’s car coming up the driveway. The kids ran out to greet him. From behind a bush across the street, a back-shooting coward named Byron De La Beckwith sighted down his .30–06 Enfield and murdered Medgar Evers in front of his family.

I parked my car and walked slowly from De La Beckwith’s sniper’s nest to the driveway at 2332 Guynes Street, tracing the route of the bullet that killed Medgar Evers on that night in 1963. I was strangely aware of every step I took. I was deeply aware of the rise and fall of my feet, one after the other. It was as though there were a tiny tremor of history beneath me the closer I came to the spot under the carport where he fell, bleeding, before his family. It felt very much like a pilgrimage to some ancient shrine. There were sad and solemn bells tolling in my mind.

Listening to Scott Colom accept his victory on Wednesday night, as he name-checked Medgar Evers, along with Fannie Lou Hamer and Hiram Revels, I heard those bells again in my mind. Loud and raucous this time, they drowned out the crack of the whip and the crack of a .30–06 Enfield rifle fired with murderous intent. This time, they rang with joy, and the tremors of history I felt beneath my feet that day in 1994 felt like an earthquake.

#6930. The Sisters Behind Dannijo Are Making Sports Merch Cool

DannijoPro was built on the idea that female fans deserve game-day gear they actually want to wear.

When Knicks frenzy overtook New York City this past summer during the team’s historic championship run, women fans—both long-suffering devotees and bandwagoners alike—went looking for something to wear. And in a city known for its fashion sense and individuality, the same old generic logo shirts and jerseys simply would not do.

DannijoPro answered the call. Seemingly overnight, the brand’s cropped blue satin bombers, bedazzled denim jackets with crochet collars, and artfully embroidered vintage sports tees took over Instagram, with celebrities like Brooke Shields, cool girls, and Ali Brunson, wife of star point guard Jalen Brunson, and Jordyn Woods, who wed center Karl-Anthony Towns shortly after he won the chip, photographed wearing DannijoPro. Knicks royalty Spike Lee even wore a DannijoPro custom jacket courtside at Game 5.

Danielle and Jodie (Danni + Jo, get it?) Snyder, the sisters and co-founders behind the bespoke fanwear company (a sporty spinoff of their original jewelry and fashion brand, Dannijo), say they were just happy to be along for the historic ride. “No one could have predicted the Knicks were going to go as far as they did, but that was amazing for us,” Jodie says of the win that ended a 53-year title drought. “People loved what we were doing.” Their Knicks finals shirts sold out three times; all 800 satin bombers (priced at $395) sold out, too. Overall, the sisters tell me their sales for 2026 are up 200 percent from last year. “I’ve never seen anything like it in my life,” says Danielle, who, along with Jodie, lived in NYC for over a decade and visited during the finals. “The energy was electric and there was such community. People were buying merch because it was so much more than a garment. It’s a piece of history. It’s a relic now.”

The Knicks run may have fueled their virality, but DannijoPro’s popularity also stems from the fact that it is serving an audience that has been ignored for too long by companies that thought that, when designing sports merch for women, it was enough to “pink it and shrink it.” And although 75 percent of women identify as fans of one or more sports, and women’s sports merch is estimated to be a $4 billion market, still a quarter of female fans say they can’t find styles they like. “It’s crazy that we’re in 2026 and you can go to a game and not find anything you want to buy as a female fan,” Jodie says.

Three months after the Knicks took home the trophy, I reach the sisters as they are gearing up for the launch of their new NBA collection for the 2026-27 season and a number of other new partnerships that have come in the wake of their viral turn, including one rolling out this month with their hometown team, the Jacksonville Jaguars—DannijoPro’s first NFL collab. Danielle is in the brand’s atelier in Tiburon in Marin County, California; as we chat on Zoom, I see people hand-stitching various garments in the background. Jodie joins the call from her car in Jacksonville, Florida, where she heads up the company’s retail and business strategy.

The sisters, who are three years apart, grew up as close as could be in Jacksonville. They’ve been creative, crafty, and entrepreneurial from a young age. Their mom, a mental health counselor, didn’t have pierced ears, but she did have a killer clip-on earring collection, and the girls were obsessed with it. In middle school, Jodie took an interest in making jewelry of her own, and Danielle, wanting to do everything her big sister did, followed right along.

Their dad is a cardiologist, and they taught themselves how to do wire work using his medical tools. Soon, they were collaborating on intricately beaded designs and selling them in local stores. “We’d see kids around school wearing our handmade designs and they didn’t even know it was us making them,” Danielle says. “What we were doing was really fresh and cool. Girls and women could take our accessories and update a white tee and jeans, or anything basic, and make it their own. That was our first taste of using style to individualize and to tell stories and we caught the bug.”

They got their big break a few years later when Danielle was in middle school. Neiman Marcus bought 150 of their turquoise nugget necklaces. “I remember staying up until 3 or 4 in the morning cranking them out,” Danielle says. “And I had an exam the next morning.” The Snyders continued to balance school and their design ambitions. Years later, when Jodie was at the University of Florida studying business and public relations and Danielle at Vanderbilt, where she majored in psychology and Spanish, Jodie would fly up and they would do trunk shows at sororities on campus. “We were always making stuff for wherever we were in life,” Jodie explains. “And everything we do is rooted in sisterhood and wanting to be together, wanting to express ourselves,” Danielle adds.

Jodie graduated from college in 2004 and moved to New York City, with Danielle following in 2007. Dannijo was founded the following year and, like the Snyders’ childhood company, quickly became known for its statement jewelry pieces. Their collection was sold in Bergdorf Goodman and Henri Bendel; characters on Gossip Girl and anchors on the Today show wore their necklaces; Beyoncé and Kim Kardashian did too (purchasing them themselves). “I remember the saleswoman calling us and saying, ‘You’re never going to believe who bought the Ruth Celia necklace,’” Danielle says. It was Queen B, who went on to wear it at Coachella, courtside at NBA games, and on a trip with her husband, Jay-Z, in Europe. “So we started getting press before we even really knew what PR was.”

They soon expanded into fashion, designing the “perfect silk slip dress” modeled after Kate Moss in the ’90s. When Jodie’s daughter was 3, she went to a tie-dye party and the next thing they knew, Dannijo was selling tie-dyed hoodies. They styled it over a slip dress and it went viral. “We sold thousands of tie-dye hoodies—it was insane,” Jodie says. “Basically, we just made things that we wanted to wear.” Collabs with Starbucks, Mercedes-Benz, and Swarovski followed.

In 2020, as the pandemic took hold in NYC, the sisters, like many residents, decamped for other parts of the country. Jodie moved back to Florida, while Danielle moved to the Bay Area. The move across the country was hard on Danielle, who was suffering from postpartum depression. “I was lost, lonely, and I didn’t have community,” she notes.

One day, her husband came home with Golden State tickets and told her they were going out. “That first Warriors game changed my life,” Danielle explains. “It changed my perspective. It got me excited again—I was excited to root for a team and to be a part of something.” They started attending games regularly and Danielle began creating her own designs to wear. “I started going through my husband’s closet and cutting up all of his old jerseys and making them into outfits for me,” she says. “My fix is always to be creative, so I was using my hands, doing stitch work, adding crystals, what have you.” Other female fans at the games began taking notice of her creative designs and asking where she had bought them. “Everyone was noticing because there was just nothing like it out there, so I called Jodie and I said, ‘This is really big.’” Soon, Dannijo’s sporty sister brand, DannijoPro, was born.

Launching a company focused on women fans was a “no-brainer,” Danielle says. “When I started making myself stuff to wear, that’s when I noticed what a crazy white space it was. Sports have never been more popular, but the product just had not caught up to that reality.” It took a year and a half to get an NBA license, but once they did, the first DannijoPro collection, which rolled out in 2024 at the Chase Center (where the Warriors play), quickly sold out.

As ever, the sisters are their own inspiration. Jodie has two children and Danielle has three. “Our worlds have changed,” Jodie says. “We’re both in T-shirts and sweatshirts; we’re not getting dressed up every day anymore.” As such, the DannijoPro items they make aren’t just for game day. They are for the 70 percent of women fans who say they’ve bought sports merch, while only 51 percent have bought tickets to attend an event. “We wanted to create items that people want to wear on and off the court or field,” Jodie says. “It's about making flattering silhouettes and clothes we all want to wear, and then applying them to the teams that we’re excited about.”

DannijoPro has three main products: officially licensed merch complete with the signature touches the designers have come to be known for, like crochet collars, hand-stitching, painting, and crystal work; “blanks” that can be customized with the help of its in-house artists (most orders come via DM); and vintage pieces that are reworked by hand.

As Jodie talks, Danielle runs around the atelier pulling out products from their new collections to show me. In August, they launched designs they made for avatars in the NBA 2K game. In September, they debuted a collab with Roger Federer’s Laver Cup tennis tournament. Their new NBA collection, called “Game Day 2.0,” rolled out last month, while their Jacksonville Jaguars line will go on sale on November 17. Future partnerships with other teams and leagues are in the works.

The sisters see their new company as “so much bigger than a fashion brand,” Danielle says. “We are reimagining fandom through a culture lens, and it’s about people. We’re living in a world of AI where everything’s like a bot. For us, it’s like, how do we bring really cool dynamic experiences in real life to people and create connections and community?”

“We think women fans should be celebrated in that there are products out there that speak to us,” Jodie adds. “It’s more than merch. I think it stands for so many other things. We can’t just accept that it’s okay to just slap logos on things, or pink it and shrink it, and women are just going to go with that. And at the end of the day, all these companies forget, but women have the buying power.”

#6929. No More Franchises

No more prequels. No more reboots. No more Harry Styles in Eternals. No more of these 71 other things.

No more movie franchises. No more shared universes. No more post-credits scenes. No more prequels. No more reboots. No more eight-hour streaming TV spin-offs. No more slates and phases. No more multiverses. No more crossovers.

No more movies that are only made to greenlight sequels. No more sequels made for getting you to sign up for a streamer that raises its monthly subscription price every year. No more Easter eggs. No more winks. No more fourth-wall breaks. No more action scenes that halt the story and are clearly directed by the second unit. No more teasers that lead to nothing. No more Harry Styles in Eternals. No more watch guides and chronological order explainers and breakdowns. No more asking, “Is it canon?”

No more four-quadrant tentpoles. No more movies based on kids’ stuff that my niece can’t actually watch. No more Superman movies that are so violent and destructive that they trigger my 9/11 survivor mother’s PTSD. No more R-rated films that sell Funkos. No more movies that have to appeal to foreign markets and all the meemaws and pawpaws in Idaho. No more quippy dialogue where characters remark, “Well, THAT just happened!” No more irony. No more grotesque violence set to upbeat pop songs. No more characters looking into their own phones. No more thudding exposition, and especially not in the first ten minutes. No more “no more romances.”

No more hiring interesting up-and-coming filmmakers with buzz coming out of Sundance and Telluride because they’re cheap and have credibility to exploit. No more boxing in those same artistes because they’ll say “yes” to all marching orders. No more rejecting established auteurs with an interesting pitch and a desire to play in the sandbox because it doesn’t fit into next summer’s tentpole. No more prioritizing release dates over craft and the well-being of everyone working on it.

No more geriatric thespians putting on spandex because today’s adult moviegoer refuses to grow up themselves. No more casting red-hot twenty-somethings and locking them into seven-movie contracts. No more firing those actors because they got into legal trouble so the audience gets gaslit into forgetting there was ever something called The Kang Dynasty. No more international talent getting nothing cool to do; no more Hiroyuki Sanada getting killed by Jeremy Renner in Avengers: Endgame. No more casting directors deciding based on their exact amount of Instagram followers. No more content creators getting cameos.

No more Comic-Con. Or rather, no more making Comic-Con into Coachella. No more sleeping on the sidewalks for a seat in Hall H. No more buzzy presentations that turn art and myth into product reveals. No more “we’re doing it for the fans” and “one more thing.” No more Oscar winners promoting movies that don’t even have a script. No more actors lying through their teeth about how they read the original comics growing up. No more trailers that shake the room for those who slept outside to get in, and then get uploaded to YouTube while they collect their sleeping bags.

No more press screenings with ridiculous embargoes. No more taking away my phone and stuffing it into a zip pouch because of a spoiler that heaven forbid leaks on TikTok when millions were spent making it in front of IMAX cameras and a unionized crew; no more thinking any of this is tantamount to government secrets. No more influencers on red carpets. No more junket “interviews” that are just quizzes and games. No more gushing. No more unprofessionalism.

No more studios that exist only to adapt in-house IP. No more C-suites rambling about the lucrative potential in old cartoons and ‘90s comic books like old-timey oil barons surveying land. No more touting the box office of one movie as evidence why they oughta make a dozen more. No more pretenses about the power of stories in earnings calls with Merrill Lynch. No more forgetting, and not giving healthy royalties to, the comic book creators whose imaginations filled the calendar slate and fueled the stock prices in the first place.

No more toxic fandoms. No more powerful studios kowtowing to the loudest, most obnoxious YouTubers who make a living screaming into microphones in their basements. No more chuds who bemoan wokeness in whatever the latest remake/sequel they’re supposed to be mad about, and use AI-generated thumbnails of themselves making the dumbest fucking faces with cherry-picked unflattering screenshots of actresses who wouldn’t give them the time of day anyway. No more abusers, sex pests, misogynists, and racists anywhere, let alone in charge.

No more ten-year plans. No more stoking tuckered-out fans to drum up excitement about the next Batman or James Bond. No more actors keeping up action figure physiques while they wait years to hear about the sequel. No more taking characters and stories people have spent their lives escaping into for comfort and deforming them into numbers on a spreadsheet. No more vague bullshit like “It didn’t capture the zeitgeist” to explain why you’re arm-swiping everything off the table again. No more using the word “zeitgeist” if you cannot define it without ChatGPT.

No more cowardice. No more promises no one can keep. No more of this circus. No more.

#6927. The Woven Tote Bag Trend Is Taking Over Runways and Workdays

Woven bags have officially reached icon status.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Is there any other bag that works as hard in real life as the tote bag? When it comes to being on duty and looking good, probably not. The humble tote has come in all iterations: lightweight cotton, supple leather, and suede. But more recently, the runways have been obsessed with making the oversize tote-style bags in luscious jumbo woven iterations. We saw them on the spring/summer 2027 runways at Dries Van Noten, Courrèges, Bottega Veneta, and Altuzarra. And then, labels like Bembien, Naghedi, and Dragon Diffusion are pumping out woven totes like there’s no tomorrow.

A big woven bag offers up texture and interest in a sea of ubiquitous canvas logo totes. It also gives you a bit of resilience so you don’t have to baby your bag on, say, a subway commute or a brisk morning walk where the risk of bumping into others is second nature. It is the working girl’s best friend. That’s just one of the reasons why fashion retailers have fallen head over heels for the style lately.

“Regardless of lifestyle, everyone needs a bag that carries a lot, and potentially the kitchen sink,” Ryan Kleman, Moda Operandi’s VP of buying, accessories, tells ELLE. The great news about a woven bag is that it also seamlessly goes with everything. Tone it down for a day at the office with a button-down top or a casual dress, or take it out for a nice dinner; a silk scarf tied about the strap can be the pop of color or embellishment you might crave for a more formal occasion.

In the case of some of these brands, the woven tote is literal fashion history. Bottega Veneta, for example, first introduced its signature “Intrecciato” woven leather technique in 1975. The fashion house’s spring/summer 2027 collection modernized the technique by applying it to enormous, hulking bags that swayed when worn over the shoulder. Paired with layered sweaters and matching leather belts, the bags felt like a decidedly fresh take on wearable artisanship by creative director Louise Trotter.

In the case of Dries Van Noten, one model carried an absolutely huge woven raffia-effect foldover clutch. Styled with a heather gray hoodie rolled up to reveal leather bubblegum-pink opera gloves with a biker-style cutout and buckle at the hand, the bag itself proved just how versatile a woven piece can be.

If you’re indeed thinking of upgrading your everyday work bag for a woven tote, it’s better to think bigger. “The oversized scale of the bags and the woven construction provide the perfect details to convert a chore into a fashion statement,” says Kleman. “At Moda, we have dubbed this trend the Big A** Bag and our customers are loving it.” This, too, is true: the bigger the weave, the better the carryall.

At the Ludlow House on the Lower East Side, a bunch of New York’s coolest people gathered to raise a glass to the essential guide to style and luxury.

It’s been two decades since the first edition of the Big Black Book debuted in 2006. Back then, a style guide devoted to the ins and outs of the luxury market wasn’t exactly common, but we believed it was necessary. The idea wasn’t to spam readers with a bunch of stuff they couldn’t afford but to give them a glimpse into the world of well-made, time-tested pieces that can create the backbone of a considered wardrobe.

Turns out, we were right. Men want to dress well, and they want to invest in the items—bench-made boots, bespoke tailoring, designer apparel—that look great on day one but even better a few years down the line. The fact that the Big Black Book is still going strong in 2026 is a testament to that, so we decided to celebrate. At the Ludlow House on the Lower East Side, we gathered a group of people who represent the BBB ethos. Sharp dressers, sure, but also sharp minds—the folks who don’t just define luxury in the moment but also lay the foundation for its future.

#6923. The Blue Tsunami Made Waves in Ways I Didn’t Expect, but I’m More Than Glad to See It

Sure, some elections touched upon the second-worst idea in American politics. But we have to take the wins when we can.

(Permanent Musical Accompaniment to This Post)

Being our semi-regular weekly survey of what’s goin’ down in the several states where, as we know, the real work of governmentin’ goes on, and where guns and clubs followed him down, all because his face was brown, better get away from Oxford town.

I’ve been busy. Did anything happen out in the states?

Anyway, the good folks at Bolts tracked an interesting rivulet of the blue wave that touches upon the second-worst idea in American politics: an elected judiciary. It seems that the momentum last week swept into state supreme court chambers.

Montana politics never cease to amaze me. The state voted for Bill Clinton twice but has gone red ever since. It had a long and proud tradition of campaign-finance legislation until Citizens United turned the table. Like all timber and mining states, Montana has a long—and fairly unique—history of labor activity. Butte became known as the “Gibraltar of Unionism.” In 1914, two factions of the powerful Western Federation of Miners went to war with each other. (The dissidents blew up the union hall in Butte.) The recent election shifted things around within that state tradition.

Similar relief spread all over the Mountain West.

And it seems possible that there’s more to come.

Meanwhile, Kansans continued to bedevil conservatives.

Speaking of Ohio, Justice Jennifer Brunner is the only Democrat on the court right now. And she has been on the right side of some very bad decisions.

As a karmic reward, Brunner will be joined by fellow Democrat Marilyn Zayas, who beat an incumbent Republican.

We have seen the importance of state supreme courts in this dismal time. Many of them threw sand in the gears of Republican gerrymandering schemes. The effect of the Democratic gains in the states is going to be deep and vast. And Deb Haaland, one of the blog’s favorite people, is the new governor of New Mexico.

And we conclude, as is our custom, in the great state of Oklahoma, whence Blog Official Escapee Friedman of the Algarve brings us a tale we can all cheer, given the events in Mississippi and elsewhere last Tuesday. From the Oklahoma Eagle:

She lived long enough to see it all. God save the good work.

This really is your democracy, America. Cherish it.

#6921. Misha & Puff’s First Womenswear Collection Gives Knitwear a New Point of View

The cult-favorite children’s knitwear brand is officially expanding to adults.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Women’s fashion has some of the best designs out there—full stop. That said, every now and then I see a well-dressed kid and think, “Damn, I wish I had something like that.” This is especially true when I see a little one sporting a design from none other than Misha & Puff, a brand known for its ethically crafted, handmade, vintage-inspired children’s clothes. And while my wardrobe carries a playful touch, I’m not necessarily looking for a supersized version of a child’s sweater, but rather the essence of that whimsy. Thankfully, I don’t have to look too far; Misha & Puff just launched its first-ever women’s collection—and it’s more than I could have hoped for.

“Knits are hot. Knits are sexy. The way that they drape, the way that they accentuate your neck, I really do feel like we can do a lot with a knit,” Sophia Roe told ELLE.com over the phone. “For me, I feel really beautiful when I put these clothes on.” The James Beard Award-winning chef—along with her daughter, Mizu—came together with artist Mara Hoffman behind the lens to create a campaign that captures the essence of Misha & Puff, highlighting the craftsmanship and expression of the brand’s new womenswear collection.

“Everything I do with the brand is very authentic and really about what I’m interested in...That’s how the kids’ brand started, with me having my son and then growing up,” Misha & Puff’s founder, Anna Wallack, said on a phone call. “The choice to launch women’s in a bigger way really comes from the pull that I’m feeling as a woman. For me, dressing women and dressing myself is about how we’re working these pieces together. How do I feel in these silhouettes? Who is this character today? I want to keep playing with that in a bigger way.”

Wallack went on to tell me that designing for the collection—which launched today—started with what she described as a “fiber-first” approach. “We work with a Pima cotton in Peru that worked so well for kids because it’s incredibly soft and it’s so wearable, and then to find that I want to wear that all the time for myself; I want to wear our merino wool,” she explained. “Our decision to launch women’s with a bigger collection really was a commitment to doing it instead of just making one sweater here and there.” The result? A range of expressive knits in a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s that encourage the wearer to be who she is as a woman today. With every solid- or heather-hued piece there’s another featuring an eye-catching print or embroidery detail. “I feel a little more permission to explore other ideas in the adult collection and what it means to be a women, too.”

This exploration extends into Misha & Puff’s campaign. Roe shared on a phone call that the idea of partnering with Misha & Puff stemmed from just being a fan of the brand. “I said, ‘I don’t know what we can do but I really want to do something with Anna.’ Little did I know that in the background she was working on this unbelievable adult collection,” she said. Though this isn’t the first project Roe has starred in with her daughter, she explained it was the first where she was shot by another mother, making the experience feel truly special—especially as she comes into her body 15 months postpartum. “I feel kind of hot as a mom. There are a lot of narratives out there about getting back to whatever that pre-baby thing is, and I just kind of rebuke that in every single way. I think what I am now is kind of amazing and I really do feel like Mara was able to bring that out in a way that no one else could have.”

On the vision for the campaign, Hoffman shared that the focus was “seeing how does a woman exist through a space or on the street, and how does she live in it?” Working to capture Roe in a documentarian way, the artist wanted to make sure the imagery felt authentic to both the brand and the people wearing it. “We are lucky to be able to capture little shots of that along the way.” Of course, shooting with a baby can come with its own unpredictability. Luckily, as Roe shared, working with someone like Hoffman helped her relax on set with the reminder, “This is just life.”
