#6919. The Audacious Southern Chef Teaching Angelenos to Shut Up and Listen

Vinyl bars like West Hollywood’s Darling, from star chef Sean Brock, will help you to appreciate music, and food, and life, all over again.

In our increasingly soulless world of algorithmic playlists and AI-generated art, one of life’s great analog pleasures is listening to records with friends. A dusty old LP pre-breaks the ice for new connections, unlocks stories you haven’t heard from old ones, gives you the chance to get collectively lost in the music.

Vinyl night is such a vital human need that it’s a surprise there aren’t more third spaces built around satisfying it. Characteristically, Japan is miles ahead of America on this front. In the country’s countless kissa bars, bartenders mix drinks and spin records, and—I swear this is true—patrons keep their voices down and listen. My partner and I saw and heard it for ourselves on a trip to Japan last year: Every major city has a few joints where the speakers are big, the tables have signs that say NO LOUD LAUGHING, and the customers obey. At a packed and reverently silent bar in Kyoto called PM Sounds, a few whiskies deep and well into the a.m., side one of Jeff Buckley’s Grace hit just right. “Did we really fly halfway around the world to sit and listen to an album we have at home?” I asked Ben on the walk back to the hotel. “Yes,” he replied, and we both agreed it was worth the trip.

Americans are famously not a quiet people, nor are we particularly good with being told what to do. Could kissa culture happen here? A few places in Los Angeles are rising to the challenge: The Gold Line in Highland Park has a hip crowd and shelves swelling with the massive record collection of owner Peanut Butter Wolf. Hollywood’s Living Room is massive and meticulously acoustically treated, and it’s currently one of the tightest doors in town. But in West Hollywood, across the street from metastasizing gay club the Abbey and steps away from the log cabin where the hip AA meetings happen, southern super chef Sean Brock just might have hit the sweet spot: His first California restaurant, Darling, has an eclectic menu, outstanding cocktails, and speakers that will make you feel like you’re right there in the recording studio. “Music brings people together, just like food,” Brock says. “I’ve been obsessed with listening bars for a while, and I’ve been trying to get people into them since 2023. The thrill of discovery is amazing, and I think the more AI the world gets, the more we’re going to reach for this stuff to balance it out.”

Brock looks every inch both the 21st-century Generation X chef and DJ: horn-rimmed glasses, sleeve tattoos, ADHD energy. He’s the James Beard Award–winning chef behind a string of beloved Nashville restaurants past and present, including modern southern standby Husk and two-in-one restaurants Audrey and June. He’s respected by foodies and the hoi polloi alike; ask anyone who did time in Nashville this century and they’ll tell you a snack at the bar of a Sean Brock joint is the reward for a hard day’s work. When we first met, just before Darling opened, we bonded over his massive collection of 45’s in the DJ booth that looks over the restaurant’s bar area. As a secular song from New Orleans preacher Bobby Powell faded out, he wanted to play me something. “Have you heard this?” It was a cover of the Mamas and the Papas’ “California Dreamin’ ” by a band from Jackson, Tennessee, called the Jacksonians. “This is all they ever did! They teased us with this and then never did another thing!” As it played, we paused, we nodded our heads. “This is the one. When people hear this, they stop talking.”

The DJ booth and its speaker cabinets were made custom for the space. “The key is the wood,” he said. “Thirteen-ply Baltic birch, which is impossible to get now.” Within the cabinets: massive Italian speakers, with Altec Lansing drivers upcycled from their first life in movie theaters.

When Brock is at Darling—generally on the weekdays if he’s not traveling—he plays records as the restaurant opens, takes over in the kitchen as the dinner rush starts, then hops back onto the ones and twos after the last dish has been served, going as long into the night as the city will let him. “Getting to play music for people after working in the kitchen all night is my reward now. For the longest time, it was getting a beer after work. Now it’s just watching music bring people happiness.”

As a record collector and a chef, Brock is always thinking about the evolution of local flavor. “What I’m chasing is the knowledge of the sound and the rhythm of a city and a time,” he told me. Take Memphis in the early ’70s, for example. “There was Willie Mitchell at Royal Studios with the Hi label doing Al Green and Ann Peebles, and you had Stax. And then there was this anger building because of the Vietnam War, people are upset, and you feel that in the music.” He grabbed a 7-inch from the pile, the gateway 45 that got him into record collecting: Blackrock’s “Yeah Yeah.” And I did feel it, because the band is a Murderers’ Row of ’70s Memphis session musicians, but mostly because of those speakers. At the right volume and in the right seat, the T. rex from Jurassic Park has nothing on Blackrock.

At its outset, Brock intended Darling to give him a new identity away from the southern cuisine that made him famous. “I didn’t want to come here and cook a bunch of stuff I’d already cooked,” he said. “And we’d been told L.A. wasn’t interested in southern cooking, that it would be too heavy or whatever. So we didn’t cook any southern dishes when we opened.” The next time I stopped in, once Darling had been up and running for a few months, that whole idea had been 86’d. “About three weeks in, everybody was like: Where are the grits, Sean?

“Now this has turned into a place where people come to taste the flavors of the South that I’ve been working on for 20 years—all the seed saving, all the research on traditions and recipes and history. I had gotten a little bored with it, honestly, but all of a sudden I have a brand-new audience for whom it’s completely new, and I’m completely reinvigorated.”

He was describing, almost exactly, the experience Ben and I had hearing Jeff Buckley in Japan. Whether it’s food or music, there’s something revelatory about paying close attention, even to something you think you know.

So that’s the food, and I can tell you from experience that the steak burger is worth the trip (and make that trip early; he makes only 24 a night). As for the music, Brock believes in the power of the listening bar with the zeal of the recent kissa convert. “I had a kind of life-changing moment in a listening bar around 2022,” he says. “I was sitting in the sweet spot—the tip of the triangle, equidistant from two gigantic Tannoys, and the sound became 3D. It floated. I stopped drinking nine years ago, but I thought they’d accidentally given me alcohol.” He got on Nashville Craigslist that very night, searched “Tannoy,” and sent a message to a seller, who wrote back, “I ate in your restaurant last night.” It’s good to be the king.

Brock has seats in the bar area of Darling situated at the perfect distance and angle from the speakers to give customers an out-of-body experience if they want it. But like a legendary southern chef distancing himself from grits, a listening bar in Los Angeles is a risky concept. In Japan, the locals and tourists know to keep quiet and focus on the music, but this is West Hollywood, where everybody wants to be the thing that is focused on. Where if people have a song selector they trust, it’s usually their Peloton instructor. As an Angeleno, I have to worry whether anyone will pipe down long enough to pay attention. “There’s a difference between a night where you come to eat and drink and socialize while music is playing and a night where we’re saying: What we’re playing is special and rare. I want to test that theory soon,” Brock told me. He’s been promoting more and more listening sessions: tributes to jazz record labels Strata-East and International Anthem; a vinyl party hosted by Sylvan Esso; Dolly Parton’s first 12 albums, in order, on those THX-tested speakers.

Even the untrained ear can hear it coming together. I swing through again to hear local legend and NTS radio host DJ Morse Code spin a set of ’70s and ’80s jazz fusion and R&B. The restaurant is busy and buzzing, there’s a nice crowd up front in the good speaker seats, at the top of the triangle. Some of the folks up front are quieting down to hear the music, even as more people stream in, even as the new arrivals are striking their poses, even as we realize that Anderson.Paak is at the bar, that Malibu cult singer-songwriter Ned Doheny is at a table in front of the DJ booth, that Kate Pierson is in the restaurant.

Darling is heating up, and the flavors, the sound, and the rhythm are continuing to evolve. The chef and the city are both learning to listen.

#6916. Time to Break Down That Major Twist at the End of ‘Campus Drivers’ and How It Differs From the Books

The Prime Video series takes a major turn from C.S. Quill’s series.

If—after watching the sixth episode of Campus Drivers—you wondered whether there were still more episodes to come, I’ve got bad news: That really was the finale. Season 1 of the French series from Prime Video ends with a twist that leaves plenty of questions, especially about Lane and Lois’s future. It’s a choice that even surprised those familiar with the book series by C.S. Quill, because the TV ending takes a different direction from the novel’s.

But what exactly happens in the final episode? And, above all, why does Lane decide to leave for Los Angeles right when his relationship with Lois finally seemed destined to take off? Let’s get into it.

In the season 1 finale, we discover that Lane leaves with his mother to move to L.A. and attend film school—a decision that, at first glance, seems to come out of nowhere, especially considering the bond that has formed with Lois over the course of the episodes. Behind this choice, however, there is a secret motivation: Lane’s mother does not approve of the path her son wants to take and believes his career choice is not the right one, but she accepts it on the condition that he leave Bellerose and end his relationship with Lois; otherwise, she is prepared to interfere with Lois’s scholarship, since she considers her an unsuitable partner for Lane.

At that point, Lane is faced with an impossible decision: stay beside the person he is falling in love with, or sacrifice his own happiness to allow her to continue studying medicine at a prestigious university.

Lane chooses the second option. He agrees to leave with his mother, thereby giving up the healthiest relationship of his life just when the two of them were finally beginning to truly grow closer.

The season’s final scene makes the separation even more concrete. In fact, we see movers carrying the furniture out of Lane’s apartment: an image that confirms his departure and the fact that the move to the United States is not just a threat or a possibility, but a decision that has now been made.

What’s more, Lois does not know the real reason for Lane’s departure. She does not know that he agreed to leave only to protect her future and allow her to continue her studies. In her eyes, that separation therefore risks seeming like a personal choice, a renunciation of their story just when it could have begun. At this point, the series departs considerably from the novel: the TV ending changes the protagonists’ journey and chooses to interrupt their story at a particularly delicate point, leaving the most important issue unresolved: the two protagonists did not get their romantic happy ending.

The ending seems designed precisely to leave the door open to a possible sequel (with the two of them still as major protagonists) and to heighten the drama—although for now, an official renewal has yet to be announced. Here’s hoping we will see Lane and Lois again in a second season!

#6915. Understated, Earthy, and Endlessly Wearable: The Best Olive Green Polishes

The earthy shade adds an edge to fall manicures while still wearing like a neutral.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like all nail polish colors, green has a vast spectrum, one that runs from electric neon to bold kelly to soft sage and all the way down to deep, moody olive. Of all the greens out there, olive sits in a quieter place. The hue is earthy and a little mysterious, somewhere between a vintage army jacket and an actual juicy olive. It’s a shade we love for fall, since it pairs so seamlessly with black, rich browns, burgundy, red, and, of course, cream.

Once reserved for the brave few, olive has now gone mainstream. Part of the appeal is how wearable it is, offering an unexpected twist without ever feeling too loud. It’s also soft and soothing to the eye, making it an easy shade to wear day after day.

If you’re new to the shade, the easiest way in is to treat it like a neutral. Try it with a camel coat and chunky knits when the weather turns, or a crisp white shirt for something fresh and unexpected. A creamy, opaque finish feels polished and modern, while a glossy jelly or a hint of shimmer adds depth in low light. However you wear it, olive looks effortlessly considered and adds an edge to your style.

Below, we’ve rounded up the best olive green nail polishes to shop now.

A personal favorite, Elia is a muted olive green that looks rich from the very first stroke. The opaque formula glides on evenly in a single swipe and dries to a soft gloss that lasts through daily wear.

Manucurist’s take on olive green feels a touch brighter. It sits beautifully on light and dark skin tones alike and delivers an opaque wash of color. Part of the “Green Flash” collection, it cures under an LED gel lamp and lasts for nearly 21 days.

Essie’s Totally Plaid is an olive green polish with a cream finish that delivers a gel-like shine and depth while still removing as easily as a regular polish. The formula applies smoothly in two coats, giving you rich, opaque color without the need for a UV lamp.

This deep and moody olive green shade is the epitome of effortless chic, pairing easily with everything from neutral knits to bold jewel tones.

Inspired by the lush landscapes of the Mediterranean, this olive green evokes a lighter take on the olive color spectrum. It’s a tad brighter and slightly more yellow-toned, making it a great pick if you want a fresh, sunlit green this fall.

O.P.I’s Suzi is the chic olive green and wardrobe staple of the olive family. It’s a bit darker-toned, radiating earthy warmth and understated elegance. Plus, it’s fast-drying and offers up to seven days of wear.

For those seeking a little more sparkle, this olive green jelly is packed with large green holographic glitters that create a textured finish and catch the light with every movement, adding a spark of joy to your manicure.

Calming and serene, this moss green shade is part of the “Campground Collection”, a four-shade assortment of earthy, comforting colors. Its buttery-smooth application matches the shade’s peaceful look.

This may not look like your average olive green at first glance, but that’s because it has taupe undertones. Once it’s on your nails, you’ll notice the hint of green was there all along. The result is a soft, grounded neutral that pairs effortlessly with cozy fall knits and earthy accessories.

This nail color may be named Pickle, but we see the olive in there. This tangy green with muted yellow undertones feels lively while remaining neutral enough to pair with everything in your wardrobe.

A true olive green, Extra Dirty brings rich, earthy depth to your nails. All you need is one to two coats for a creamy manicure worthy of a fall-season spotlight.

#6913. 12 12 12 Shakes Up the Tired Heist Formula with One Great Idea

Can the Anthony Mackie-led crime drama pull it off?

“A high-asset robbery, professionally conceived, is twelve months in planning, twelve hours in execution,” says Anthony Mackie’s character, FBI special agent Jack Strickland, at the start of the new Apple TV crime drama, 12 12 12. “A bank robber who is not caught within twelve days is not caught at all.”

It’s an amazing premise—honestly, one of the best I’ve heard in a long time. The first episode takes the viewer one month into planning the heist, one day into pulling off the heist, and one hour into their attempted escape. Then, episode 2 will move along to two months, two days, and two hours. Neat, right? But the big question for cocreators Jim Keeble and Dudi Appleton’s time-shifting heist series remains the same as any seemingly impossible bank job: Do they have the right team to pull it off?

From the two-episode premiere—now available to stream on Apple TV—I’d say we’re off to a very strong start. 12 12 12’s opening scene pays homage to Ocean’s 11’s Shaobo Qin when a contortionist unfolds his body and climbs out of a large safety deposit box. It’s one of the most genius ideas I’ve seen in a heist story of the last few years—I mean, talk about a man on the inside—especially in an overcrowded field of complex heist series. Remember Netflix’s Kaleidoscope, which allowed viewers to watch the story starting on any episode in the series? Well, 12 12 12 is far more than just a gimmick.

The story follows FBI special agent Jack Strickland (Mackie), who has tracked down one of the world’s best bank robbers, Paul Galvin (Jamie Dornan), to Zurich, Switzerland. Galvin and his unruly brother Bobby (Jack Kesy, who was last seen playing Robbie Pedergrast’s late brother on Task), made an enemy of Strickland when the FBI agent’s partner was killed as she attempted to arrest them during a major heist in Newark, New Jersey.

Now, Strickland has a personal vendetta against the man. He throws his philosophy that you’ll never catch a bank robber after twelve days of the robbery out the window—which is fine because the creators fabricated the whole “12 12 12” FBI rule anyway just to provide episodic framing for the series. (Maybe it resets now that Galvin is planning another heist?) Either way, 12 12 12 does a great job of making sure you never get lost in the three timelines.

Strickland’s white whale is also far from a one-note character himself. He’s a man following in his father’s footsteps to get out of a life of poverty, which would be easier if his brother Bobby wasn’t such of a wild card who can’t be trusted unattended for a single second.

As it turns out, Bobby gambled away all their money from the Newark heist, including Paul’s half. So his brother’s debtors force Paul back into a life of crime under threat of violence to his new lady love in France, his father-in-law, and their unborn child. It’s also quickly revealed that our contortionist is just one of the interesting members of Paul’s team, who are all tasked by Bobby’s mysterious benefactors with robbing a Zurich bank of 800,000 Swiss francs all owned by the Russian mafia.

From the first two episodes released so far, the 12 12 12 team seems like it has the right men for the job. The timeline-shifting format allows the series to do much more than simply break the action to provide a little backstory. As we move from months ahead to days after, and vice-versa, we also switch perspectives that allow for Paul and Jack to trade who is the hero and who is the villain.

Jack can be both the righteous FBI agent pursuing the bank robbers in the present and a man clearly far too hellbent on revenge in the past. The same goes for Paul, whose team blackmails, honeypots, and occasionally murders throughout the heist, while his backstory paints him as a good man who was forced down a dark road.

Where 12 12 12 falls short, at least so far, is in the script. Kari Skogland (who previously worked with Mackie on The Falcon and the Winter Soldier) directs the two-part premiere handily enough, but Keeble and Appleton’s writing is very formulaic. Scripts don’t always matter when you’re working with amazing characters like the contortionist lockpick. True Detective: Night Country’s Kali Reis is also on the team. Her mission is to hack into the subway system so that they can make their escape on public transit, which, of course, goes wrong. But when you have so much dialogue doubling as exposition in between the action, it feels like everyone’s conversations are more for the viewer than they are for each other.

For now, I’m hopeful that all the kinks are worked out once we move past the who, what, and why of 12 12 12’s opening episodes. Given the gimmick, and the fact that the series is only eight episodes instead of twelve, I’m excited to see just what throws the entire concept of the series into chaos.

#6910. Here’s How Much the Mirrorball Trophy Is Worth on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

We’re just a few weeks out from the Dancing With the Stars season 35 finale (it goes down on November 24), which means one couple is about to walk away with the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy. You know, the giant disco ball that everyone hoists above their heads after winning?

And if you’re wondering how much it’s worth, the answer is PRICELESS, obviously. But also, kind of a lot.

The mirrorball itself is incredibly cheap. Production designer James Yarnell told People in 2017, “No matter how much we put into the trophy, how much we spend, it always has a $10 mirrorball on top.” The show does throw down for the base, however. Winner Kaitlyn Bristowe told Trading Secrets that, all together, the trophy is worth around $10,000.

Apparently Hollywood 3D Printing—who uses “3D printing technologies combined with advanced finishing capabilities such as detailed painting, electroplating, and vacuum plating”—is responsible for making the Mirrorball Trophies, which they confirmed on Instagram in 2023.

Meanwhile, this glitzy statue has come a long way since the early days of Dancing With the Stars. Yarnell said he made the original Mirrorball Trophy (which Kelly Monaco won) from a brass lamp, a wooden base, and some tape. And it stayed that way for four full seasons! Per Yarnell, “They stopped making the lamp at Lamps Plus, so we’d run out of options and had to start casting it and put a heavy acrylic base with a brass stem.”

As for whether or not celebs and pros keep their trophies, it presumably depends on the person. But Derek Hough is so attached to his collection that he literally travels with them on tour and puts them in the lobby for fans to enjoy.

#6909. The 6 Best Milk Frothers for Coffee Aficionados

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These handheld and automatic machines will give you the foamy upgrade you need.

With the increasing prices of, well, everything, it’s more important than ever to save in all the little places you can. For the caffeine addicted, making your daily beverage at home is an easy place to start. And you can pretty easily trick yourself into thinking your home coffee is as good as your neighborhood café when you invest in a milk frother. Frothed milk is certainly not a necessity for making good coffee, but it is a requirement for any half-decent latte or espresso drink. If you've already got the basics, like fresh beans, a good coffee machine, a grinder you love, and something to drink the coffee out of, a milk frother is the final piece of the puzzle. At last, you can call yourself a home coffee connoisseur.

All milk frothers essentially work the same way, by aerating the milk to make varying degrees of bubbles. Milks with higher fat content make a silkier froth; lower-fat milks (and nondairy milks) make for airier froth. If you have an espresso machine, you can make a cappuccino or latte, or you can top your regular coffee (or matcha) with foam for a café au lait. It’s mostly science, but you can inject as much artistry as you want to the process depending on which route you go.

There are more options than you think on the market. You can go with something handheld, automatic, or manual. Some let you control the amount of foam made and dial in the texture you want; others have presets that do the deciding for you. Some have settings dedicated to plant milk. No matter your coffee preferences, you’ll find something that will fit you with these six top-notch milk frothers.

Instant Pot might not be the first name you think of when you think coffee, but this affordable gadget will change that. The MagicFroth is easy to use, with two buttons that let you set the temperature and froth level between low, medium, and high settings. This makes it easy to get the hang of, although it does lack the depth and nuance that some more expensive machines offer.

One of the big advantages of any frother of this style is it can be used to heat up milk for other beverages like hot chocolates or a matcha latte. With that in mind, this is a pretty tremendous value. Plus, the stainless-steel pitcher, plastic lid, and whisk all are dishwasher-safe, making cleanup as easy as humanly possible.

If you’re really dedicated to the idea of a milk frother, you probably want something that looks good on your countertop. The Smeg mini frother is a one-button smooth operator that has that striking retro ’50s look we’ve come to expect (and sometimes lose our minds over) from the brand. You can choose between hot froth, cold foam, or a warm milk mode, and those are the only choices. That can be a pro or a con depending on what you want from your frother. If you value aesthetics and simplicity, you can’t go wrong with this single-serve milk frother.

We praised this handheld, travel-friendly hand frother in this year’s Esquire gadget awards. It’s small and dead quiet, but has enough power to it to make a decent little milk foam. Plus, it can be quickly disassembled into the storage case to either be safely put away for later or packed into your luggage.

It foams—it can even be used as a whisk or mixer with the other two of its three attachments. Use it to whisk your eggs or blend your protein shake in the morning. It can handle it all, and once it runs out of juice just plug it in with a USB-C cable to recharge it in a matter of minutes.

People love their Nespressos, and the Aeroccino matches the performance and aesthetic of most machines. If you have an original Nespresso or Vertuo machine and want to elevate your pod drinks with some latte art, well, this is the next step. Four different touch buttons get you to any of the four settings: cold frothed milk, airy frothed milk, silkier frothed milk, and just straight up warmed milk. It’s just the one compartment, so it’s not dishwasher safe. You’ll be cleaning by hand and rinsing by hand between batches too. It’s a little more hassle than some of these other machines in the long run.

If you have a family of latte lovers, you might need something with a bit more capacity for foaming multiple drinks’ worth of milk at once. That something is the stainless steel Breville Café Milk Frother. It’s a high-capacity frother that can hold about double what the average one does.

For control freaks, Breville's milk frother has the most amount of options for you to play with. You can dial in the exact temperature of your foam, which most other models don't let you do. Plus, the top half comes out, and you can just put it in the dishwasher. Despite its size, this makes it easier to clean than pretty much any other model out there.

If, like me, you just can’t do whole milk in your lattes anymore (or ever), you want a machine that will do just as good a job with any alternative. Be it oat, almond, or any plant milk of your choice, you want to be able to put anything in your frother and have it whip up a delicious foam. This De’Longhi frother has a button dedicated to it (as well as one dedicated to cold foam). It also happens to be the second-best-looking option on this list.

#6907. Zimmermann Hosts a Star-Studded Soirée in Downtown New York City

Katie Holmes, Uma Thurman, and Naomi Watts came out to celebrate the brand’s cruise 2027 collection.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Inside Indochine’s red-lit, retro-inspired French-Vietnamese dining room in New York City, Zimmermann gathered friends of the brand to celebrate its cruise 2027 collection. Presented on the sun-drenched Côte d’Azur in June, the label’s latest served as the perfect bit of vacation and occasion-ready dressing for November in New York. Now that the lineup has officially hit stores, fashion insiders and fans of the brand alike (including Uma Thurman, Eve Hewson, Katie Holmes, and Naomi Watts) showed up dripping in ruffled gowns, sailor-inspired stripes, chunky shearlings, and ethereal lace and silks from the collection. Against the dark, 1940s-inspired tropical salon interior of Indochine, it was the ideal mix of downtown energy and New York glamour.

As champagne flowed through the bar area, glasses clinked while miniature crab cakes and spring rolls were passed. Zimmermann’s sister duo Nicky and Simone Zimmermann could be spotted catching up with friends new and old. “We have a very, very close connection with New York,” Nicky told ELLE in between greeting guests. “It was pretty much when I started the business, one of the first places internationally that I went to in the ’90s, and it blew my mind. It’s been inspirational to us 100 percent.” It was a rare occasion for the brand; for years, Zimmermann has shown at Paris Fashion Week (after presenting in New York in the mid-2010s) and the duo oscillates between Sydney and Paris for most of the year.

Zimmermann’s painterly and intrinsically bohemian flourishes could be seen in the many outfits of guests, all highlighting the vivid cruise collection. Think: decadent silky draped drop-waisted gowns and paneled lace maxi dresses, or, for the more demure, sleek all-black looks that captured the New York essence. “For me, there’s a little bit of a sense of humor behind it—without insulting anybody—it was about the story when Australia actually beat America in the America’s Cup, which is a very famous sailing race,” Nicky told ELLE of the collection’s inspiration.

“I think it was in 1983, and America had not lost the America’s Cup in something like 173 years; it was a ridiculous winning streak, and Australia came along. The inspiration is more about a David and Goliath kind of story,” she continued. “We came along as the Aussies, not established in the sailing world. They were very cheeky, rogue, kind of Aussie young guys up against this amazing, sophisticated establishment of the New York Yacht Club. And they never lost—and we beat them. So in Australia, it’s a legendary story.” Nicky combined the sportiness of sailing gear with the sort of elite New York Yacht Club preppiness, resulting in colorful silk scarf embellishment or sweaters decked out in accessorized pins. “They would hold the most incredible parties and the women were so glamorous and sophisticated and beautiful and not anything that Australia could relate to at the time, so I loved the contrast.”

Conversation was flowing and blooming anthuriums covered the tables as guests took their seats. Nicky kicked off the dinner with a speech, musing on her early days in New York on the Lower East Side and the many parties she attended in Tribeca before sharing more inspiration behind the collection: “It’s about establishment, and then it’s about people, you know, working really hard, coming together as a team, defeating the undefeatable,” she said, with a laugh.

Next came an intimate performance by the singer and Bad Monkey actress Charlotte Lawrence, who sang two of her original songs, then led a folksy sing-along to “Dreams” by Fleetwood Mac. Even the most guarded of celebs and fashion’s elite couldn’t help but bob their heads and sing a tune. As guests dined on a family-style mix of kale salad, spicy shrimp, grilled salmon, barbecued chicken, and steak, the champagne glass clinks slowed as candles slowly flickered and the evening closed out with lemon tarts.

A little yacht-club polish, a little Aussie irreverence: Zimmermann celebrated cruise 2027 in high style.

#6906. Second Wave Delivery Systems: DRG and Clinical Appeals Specialist

This position is designed as a cross-functional revenue integrity role. While primary responsibilities focus on clinical and DRG appeals, the specialist is expected to support DRG validation audits, coding compliance reviews, clinical validation initiatives, and denial-prevention activities during periods of lower appeals volume. Flexibility and the ability to work across multiple revenue cycle disciplines are essential requirements of the position.

Review denied claims involving DRG downgrades, clinical validation and coding denials.

Conduct a comprehensive analysis of medical records, coded data, physician documentation, laboratory results, imaging findings, treatments, and patient outcomes to identify appeal opportunities.

Draft clear, persuasive, evidence-based appeal letters using clinical indicators, coding guidelines, payer policies, Coding Clinic guidance, and CMS regulations.

Prepare first-level and second-level appeals and reconsideration requests for commercial, Medicare, Medicare Advantage, and Medicaid payers.

Defend the clinical validity of diagnoses commonly targeted by payers, including sepsis, acute respiratory failure, acute kidney injury, malnutrition, encephalopathy, and other MCC/CC conditions, using evidence-based practice and literature.

Validate coding accuracy, DRG assignment, principal diagnosis selection, present-on-admission indicators, and reimbursement determinations.

Research and incorporate peer-reviewed clinical literature, specialty society guidance, and regulatory references to support appeal positions.

Track appeal outcomes, recovered revenue, overturn rates, and denial trends for reporting and continuous improvement.

Participate in client education initiatives focused on denial prevention, documentation improvement, and coding accuracy.

Maintain productivity during fluctuations in denial volume by independently conducting DRG audits, coding compliance reviews, clinical validation assessments, pre-bill revenue integrity reviews, and denial-prevention initiatives to support organizational revenue cycle objectives

Current RN license or professional coding credential required.

Bachelor’s degree in nursing, Health Information Management, or a related healthcare field preferred.

Minimum of five years of experience in Clinical Documentation Integrity (CDI), inpatient coding, denials management, clinical validation, DRG validation, or revenue cycle operations.

Minimum of three years of experience writing successful payer appeals preferred.

Experience reviewing Medicare, Medicare Advantage, Medicaid, RAC, and commercial insurance denials.

Demonstrated experience working with electronic health record (EHR) systems and encoder applications.

Proficiency with 3M, Epic, Cerner, TruCode, Optum, or similar CDI or coding technologies preferred.

One or more of the following certifications preferred: CCDS, CDIP, CCS, RHIA, RHIT, CPC, or CRC.

Advanced knowledge of ICD-10-CM/PCS coding guidelines, MS-DRG and APR-DRG methodologies, and reimbursement systems.

Strong understanding of clinical documentation integrity principles and clinical validation standards.

Exceptional analytical and critical-thinking skills, with the ability to interpret complex clinical records.

Excellent written communication skills, including the ability to develop concise, persuasive, and technically sound appeal letters.

Knowledge of CMS regulations, payer audit methodologies, Coding Clinic guidance, and industry best practices.

Ability to prioritize workload independently and

Demonstrated ability to function effectively in a flexible, cross-trained role, transitioning between denials and appeals activities, DRG validation, coding compliance auditing, clinical validation reviews, and other revenue integrity functions based on business needs

Clinical Expertise: Interprets clinical indicators, treatment patterns, and disease processes to support diagnosis validation.

Appeals Writing: Develops well-supported, evidence-based arguments designed to maximize overturn success rates.

Analytical Thinking: Identifies weaknesses in denial rationales and opportunities for reimbursement recovery.

Collaboration and Flexibilty: Partners effectively with physicians, CDI teams, coding professionals, and revenue cycle stakeholders.

Regulatory Knowledge: Applies CMS regulations, coding guidance, and payer policies accurately and consistently.

Results Orientation: Focuses on maximizing recovered revenue and reducing recurring denial patterns.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.

#6904. The ‘Campus Drivers’ Finale May Have Revealed Who Season 2 Will Be About, and It’s a Change From the Books

Lois and Lane’s unfinished story could signal a major shift.

The season 1 finale of Campus Drivers left viewers with several questions, one of them being: Who will the protagonists of the next season be? Prime Video’s French-language series is based on the book series by C.S. Quill, but it has already demonstrated that it doesn’t necessarily intend to follow the novels’ path to the letter. And the very way Lane and Lois’s story ends could foreshadow an important narrative choice for the future of the adaptation.

As a reminder: In the series finale, Lane decides to leave for Los Angeles with his mother in order to allow Lois to keep her scholarship. Their story is cut short just when it seemed destined to turn into something more, leaving several questions open that could become central in a second season.

However, season 1 does also end with a twist involving Donovan. Without giving too much away about what happens in the second book, Crash Course, which is centered on Donovan, the ending sees (spoiler!) Donovan’s dad collapse during a basketball practice. Still, Lois and Lane remain at the center of the narrative with a story still entirely unresolved…and this choice may not be accidental.

The choice to keep Lane and Lois at the center of the story could suggest that Campus Drivers will not necessarily follow the narrative model of Off Campus. In the series based on Elle Kennedy’s novels, each season is designed to mainly tell the story of a new couple, giving space to different protagonists within the same narrative universe.

For Campus Drivers (despite the series having the same structure), the direction could be different: keeping Lane and Lois as central figures in the coming seasons as well, gradually developing the love stories of the other characters and giving them more and more space. Based on the season 1 ending, it’s possible that it will instead use an increasingly ensemble structure, in which the protagonists’ stories intertwine and become richer episode after episode, without necessarily making way for a new main couple each season.

This does not rule out the possibility that, further down the line, other characters could take on an even more important role. As early as the third season, for example, there could be room to explore the secondary love stories in greater depth. For now, however, these are only hypotheses; Prime Video has not yet confirmed what the structure of any future chapters will be.

If the series is renewed, it is reasonable to expect Lane and Lois to maintain a central role. The first season finale left their relationship hanging, and it would be difficult to imagine a follow-up that does not pick up precisely from what happened between them—especially because six episodes felt like too few to fully develop a dynamic between the leads.

The choice not to follow the structure of the novels could also be a way to differentiate Campus Drivers from Off Campus, especially since both series share the same platform and target the same audience of fans.

At the moment, a second season has not yet been officially confirmed. However, considering the attention the series is receiving and the narrative potential left open by the finale, it would not be surprising to see new updates arrive soon.

#6903. MobLand Episode 9 Sets Up a Seriously Violent Finale

In season 2’s penultimate episode, Harry faces down Frankie to save Gina, while Kat McAllister calls Black Flag on the Harrigans.

Consider Tom Hardy’s smile. I know you’ve got a lot to think about right now, like the rising cost of a flat white, celebrity sex criminals, and which brand of pasta to stockpile, but put that on ice for a minute and consider Tom Hardy’s smile.

I could count on one hand the number of times I’ve seen him crack a grin on screen. He’s like those actors who’ll go nude only if it’s tasteful and in the name of art. Hardy’s smiles are anything but tasteful. When they happen, it’s as surprising as any jump scare.

Take episode 9 of MobLand, when Harry gloriously threatens Frankie. “That fucking face of yours, I’ll ‘ave that,” he growls like Burglar Bill’s psychopath cousin. “I’ll wear it to carnival as a mask when I cut it off with this pint glass.”

The eyebrows arch and the lips creak upward and the face cracks like a dam with a hairline fracture, exploding into a horrifying clown grin. Then… suddenly it’s gone, like a split atom, back into the cosmic darkness from whence it came. They say the best actors can tell a story with their eyes. Hardy can tell one with just his teeth, and it’s brilliant and it’s terrifying.

Anyway, that was my highlight of episode 9.

What else? Well, the cold open, where Frankie gatecrashes Kat’s ambassadors’ convention disguised as a waiter to threaten her. Kat is ready: “Under the 1961 Vienna Convention, unlawful entry into a sovereign embassy is not seen as trespass but as an attack on the nation. I call Black Flag and you do not make it off this fucking rug.”

Talking of smiles, Gina wipes one clean off Maeve’s face with an exit straight out of RuPaul’s Drag Race. You’ll recall Maeve’s dastardly scheme: persuade Gina to fake a suicide attempt, thus breaking Harry’s spirit and forcing him out. Instead, Gina sashays away, leaving a message in lipstick on the mirror: “Suck my balls, witch.”

Meow. I don’t think Ru could have put it better herself.

Maeve turns back to Eddie. “You want to be a Harrigan, there’s one last task: Harry.”

It’s not long before the smile returns to Maeve’s face, when she wakes to find Conrad in his pants, strapping on a bulletproof vest. “What are you doing, man? It’s not Friday. Friday night is bondage night!”

Actually, Conrad has pried a tasty piece of intel from JoJo, the Frankie henchman he tortured to death: a large shipment of drugs is landing at Tilbury Docks, ripe for the nicking. Off they drive with a boot full of weapons, soundtracked not by a true-crime podcast but by the bloodcurdling recording of JoJo’s torture.

Trouble is, just as they arrive, the ghouls of Conrad’s past start stalking the corridors of his mind again, whispering. “Leave me alone,” he cries, swerving the car. Maeve shows an almost uncharacteristic amount of empathy. “It’s okay,” she soothes.

Still, the heist is off. Conrad is in no fit state to add to the ranks of the ghosts he’s made. They drive home.

If there were an award for the weirdest scene of the series, it would go to Eddie asking Paul on a date. “Wondered if you fancied popping out for a pint later.” He might as well have asked him to go dogging in a Chipping Norton lay-by.

Really, he wants a bomb to kill Harry. Paul laughs it off with a monologue on the history of bombmaking, until Eddie plays his ace: “What if I tell you I know it was you who told Harry that I was in Ireland? I wonder what Maeve would think.” Paul’s smile dissolves. Maeve would have his guts for sausages. Or, in Eddie’s words: “How does it feel to have your bollocks in my briefcase?”

And so to the meat and potatoes of the episode: Harry and Frankie’s tête-à-tête.

It starts at Harry’s home. Jan has returned to him, having realized she’s safer in his giant hands than in anyone else’s. He wants to whisk her to a safehouse, urgently. So she does what anyone would do when their life is in imminent danger: run a bath, throw on some Damien Rice and soak her troubles away.

Trouble is, those troubles are literally downstairs with assault rifles, with Frankie close behind. Naturally, Harry keeps an even bigger gun under the sofa, so he festoons the walls of his home with their brains before leading Jan out to the car. Gina has just arrived. So, alas, has Frankie.

Still in his dressing gown, Frankie kidnaps Gina and spirits her off to the panic room/prison cell beneath his London home.

There, Gina and Frankie bond over a spot of smoothie play, hurling their drinks in each other’s faces, followed by a round of Nerf. It’s sweet. He likes her. Maybe he even gets her. She is, as he later tells Harry, “spunky, full of spunk.” That may just be what keeps her alive.

Over at Kevin HQ (a shipping container on an industrial estate), Harry, Jan and Kevin await news of Gina. Kevin pitches a new plan: go John Wick on the lot of them.

“You call Frankie and tell him we accept on the condition he returns Gina safely… then we meet Conrad and Maeve and blow their fucking brains out.” Then Eddie, then Frankie, then, through Bella’s nous, defenestrate Kat McAllister via legal channels.

Conrad, whose marbles have been rolling under the sofa all episode, is not expected to put up much of a fight.

“I’m taking over tonight,” fumes Kevin. “You’re either with me or against me.”

Well, no need for that just yet. Harry has a plan to save Gina. Leverage? Another child he can senselessly murder? No, no. Simply the good old-fashioned threat of violence.

At the parley, they’re yin and yang: Frankie the exuberant chatterbox, Harry the man who’ll never use two words when one will do. Frankie squeezes Harry’s arm: “Cor, feel those muscles. I mean, I’m tasty, but if we got in an actual ruck, I wouldn’t stand a snowflake. I bet you could kill me with your bare hands…”

“You’re not gonna kill her,” he growls wearily.

“You mind testing that?” And Frankie orders a goon to blow Gina’s head off with a shotgun and film it so they can watch.

He hands Harry the phone for the tersest of chats with his gaffer-taped daughter: “Try to stay calm. I’m gonna get you out of this as soon as I can.” Click. Frankie can’t believe it. “Is that it? Unless you’ve got some brilliant plans, that is actually a bit shit. What are you up to, Harry?”

Harry’s reply is considered and simple: “If I was you, I would’ve had some really big, scary fucking geezers in here with you to keep you safe, to prevent the exact scenario that is unfolding right here.”

“B-b-b-but I’ve just got to pick up the phone,” stammers Frankie.

The tension is suddenly tighter than a celebrity sex criminal’s grip on the prison soap.

Harry’s purring now: “It took me seven seconds to walk through those doors from the car… No more cutting around in the old burgundy tracksuit, that’s for sure. Not without wearing a fucking colostomy bag hanging off your limp arse.”

Then the scary smile, the face-mask line and the clincher: “Speaking of seven seconds, that’s all you’ve got left to call your mate Joey and let Gina go. Three... two...”

But Frankie has one more trick up his tracksuit: he gets Kat McAllister on the phone. Remember the black flag stuff at the top of the episode?

She’s offering Harry another chance: “As of tonight there is no Harrigans. But the good news: you have a new job. Thank you for what you’re about to do for us. I am looking forward to you working for the team. Black Flag, motherfucker.”

In other words, Black Flag has been called, and the Harrigans are its target. It’s a fitting phrase. Pirates flew a black flag to tell ships to surrender or else. In motor racing, it means you’re disqualified. And it’s a snarling late-seventies hardcore punk band, whose “Nervous Breakdown” gives this episode its title. Kat’s version is all three at once.

Cut to the Cotswolds, where police are dredging the lake. They’ve pulled out a body. Is it Richie’s? It’s someone’s, and I’d wager they didn’t die smiling. Hardy, on the other hand, lives to smile another day.

The chips are down, the traps are laid, and there’s one episode left. Does Frankie have one last plan up his tracksuit? Will Conrad pull his brain back from the brink? Will Maeve get the last laugh? Or will it be Harry, God help us all?

As Shakespeare puts it in Macbeth: “There’s daggers in men’s smiles: the near in blood, the nearer bloody.”

#6902. Culture Watch: What to See and Where to Go in November

Culture Watch is a series spotlighting the intersection of fashion and art, bringing you the latest must-see exhibitions, pop-ups, installations, and more.

November is a good month to make a little room in your calendar. As the holiday season starts to gather pace, galleries, brands, and creative spaces are offering plenty of reasons to get out and look around. In New York, Salon Art + Design returns for its 15th year, while Loro Piana presents its “Master of Cashmere” installation at The Brant Foundation; farther afield, international exhibitions offer even more to explore. Check out all of this month’s best cultural happenings below.

Louis Vuitton Presents ‘La Valise Bruno Aveillan’ at LV Dream

For 13 years, photographer and filmmaker Bruno Aveillan has taken his Louis Vuitton Alzer suitcase to more than 100 destinations, making it a recurring presence in his work. Now, the well-traveled case is at the center of “La Valise Bruno Aveillan,” an immersive exhibition at LV Dream, the house’s encompassing destination in Paris. Aveillan’s photographs appear alongside historic Louis Vuitton trunks and a closer look at the craftsmanship behind the Alzer, the brand’s hard-sided suitcase built around a sturdy wooden frame. The exhibition marks the final chapter in the house’s yearlong celebration of the monogram’s 130th anniversary.

Loro Piana Names Its Cashmere of the Year and Unveils a ‘Master of Cashmere’ Installation

Loro Piana put cashmere in the spotlight at The Brant Foundation in New York, where it marked the 11th edition of its Cashmere of the Year Award. Established in 2015 and held stateside for the first time, the prize recognizes the herders who produce the finest cashmere fiber. Coinciding with the honor, which went to three couples of cashmere herders from Inner Mongolia, is “Master of Cashmere: The Quest for Fiber Excellence,” an installation that traces the material from its origins to the Maison’s work with it. The multi-room experience features archival sketches, explanatory timelines, and, of course, soft-to-the-touch cashmere.

Salon Art + Design Returns to the Park Avenue Armory for Its 15th Year

The 15th edition of Salon Art + Design is now on view at the Park Avenue Armory—and it’s one of its most immersive, expansive editions yet. As is tradition, the event brings together collectible design, fine art, and jewelry. The event is open through November 8. Standouts include Jean Dunand’s 1929 La Forêt lacquered screen, Michel Dufet’s 1918 daybeds, and Jean Royère’s illuminated desk. Fernando Mastrangelo’s totem lamps add a sculptural note, while the jewelry lineup includes pieces from Fernando Jorge, Mellerio, Cora Sheibani, and Viltier.

Lara Saget Debuts Her First New York Solo Show, ‘And the World Came Apart’

Lara Saget brings more than a decade of material experimentation to her first solo exhibition in New York. At Trotter&Sholer’s new Elizabeth Street space, her sculptures and wearable works fuse glass, stone, and metal through heat, pressure, and time. Drawing on geological processes and NASA data, the show moves from ancient natural formations to humanity’s rapid impact on the planet.

The Phillips Collection Opens ‘Avery, Gottlieb & Rothko: By the Sea’

Milton Avery went to Gloucester, Massachusetts, to “live like an artist,” then brought Adolph Gottlieb and Mark Rothko along for summers of creative exchange. In Washington, D.C., The Phillips Collection’s “Avery, Gottlieb & Rothko: By the Sea” brings together watercolors and color-drenched canvases, tracing what those Cape Ann days set in motion.

Tate St Ives Spotlights Wilhelmina Barns-Graham

What does a landscape feel like in the body? Scottish artist Wilhelmina Barns-Graham spent eight decades exploring that question through art shaped by sensation and memory. The retrospective at Tate St Ives in Cornwall, England, is the most comprehensive survey of her work, moving from early landscapes and studio interiors to later works alive with bold color and sweeping gestures.

Taylor Swift, take note: famously hard-to-get-into New York City hotspot The Corner Store has officially landed at The Cosmopolitan of Las Vegas, marking its first-ever location outside Manhattan. Expect its adventurous martinis, signature wood-paneled interiors by Rockwell Group, and a handful of Vegas-exclusive menu additions, including a Butcher’s Counter serving up wagyu tomahawk and porterhouse steaks.

#6898. These 9 Best Waxed Canvas Jackets Are Good-Looking and Hard-Wearing

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Nothing is better suited for cold, wet weather.

Why did we ever stray from this path? A good waxed canvas jacket will handle rough weather, sure—any new-age rain jacket can do that as well. But the magic of this cloth is the look. Made from heavy-duty cotton canvas treated with an oil-based waxed coating, these jackets offer a barrier against rain and snow while also blocking the wind—but they look damn good doing it.

Once you have a waxed canvas jacket, it becomes uniquely yours, developing a weathered patina with every wear. And if you give it a fresh coat of wax every year or so, these jackets will stay waterproof and last for most, if not all, of your life.

Here, we’ve rounded up our favorite styles from the names you’d expect, like Barbour, Filson, and L.L. Bean, alongside a few brands you might not know yet. Ready to brave the unexpected fall and winter weather? With one of these jackets, you will be.

An enduring icon thanks to its tenacious popularity, the Barbour Beaufort waxed jacket boasts all the classic details created by Dame Margaret Barbour, including a color-contrast corduroy collar (which you can turn up and secure when it gets cold), paneled tartan lining, flapped bellow pockets, and a two-way front zip. It’s truly an evergreen piece that will never go out of style, and Style Director Jonathan Evans argues that if you’re going to invest in a waxed canvas jacket, it should be the Beaufort.

For a chic, elevated look (pretty hard to achieve in rainy weather), Tom Beckbe’s Tensaw is a great pick. The Alabama-based brand has that hunting tradition, but it doesn’t go full upland. It’s more elevated than that.

Since 1948, the Trialmaster jacket has been Belstaff's signature style, its origin going back to the Scottish Six Days Trial, a motorcycle competition that attracts riders worldwide. Developed to keep the competitors dry in tough weather conditions, it’s built with black waxed cotton and features a legacy slanted map pocket and an adjustable belt, so you can always find the perfect fit.

Made from Filson’s signature waxed cotton, this jacket has protected foresters and outdoorsmen from brambles and abrasion in the rain-soaked forests of the Pacific Northwest since the 1920s—talk about an endorsement. The hip-length cut allows for easy access to pockets or a tool belt, and this type of fit will also stop the jacket from riding up when you’re seated.

For a super-warm option, Flint and Tinder’s flannel-lined style ensures you won’t feel a shiver or a sprinkle. Tough, timeless, and made in the U.S.A., it’s constructed with a weather-resistant Martexin seven-ounce sailcloth, which, like selvage denim or finely tanned leather, will only get better with age. It’s more lightweight than denim or leather, but still substantial enough to feel durable. Take it from our endorsement of the jacket:

“It’s not even like a techy Arc’teryx shell you might want to be more careful with because of the weight. This is the kind of jacket you don’t think twice about wearing. You wear it in the rain, not worrying if it brushes against brick walls. You toss it in the backseat of your car—or, even better, the backseat of a side-by-side on a weekend in the woods. Over time—years, I mean—the fabric picks up creases. They don’t ruin it. They define it. It becomes yours.”

The first thing you’ll notice about this jacket is that it’s actually true to size and has a great fit. It leaves just enough room for layering without the risk of looking sloppy, and it’s slim enough without feeling too restrictive. We also loved how comfortable it is and like that the weathered fabric gives it a classic vintage appeal (without having to spend hours searching a secondhand shop).

TBC doesn’t just make damn good boots—the brand has perfected the durable, rugged, highly functional jacket that doesn’t sacrifice style for comfort. We like how the water-resistant finish only gets better with age and how the polyester sleeve lining both allows for ease of movement and prevents the sleeves from bunching up. Plus, it’s great for colder days thanks to the warm blanket flannel lining.

Tecovas keeps it cool, casual, and authentic with a waxed canvas ranch jacket that makes an everyday outfit look even better than it would sans jacket. Whether you’re mending fence posts or making a quick dash to the office or pub, this jacket will help muzzle the nip in the air and keep downpours at bay. It’s lightweight and less bulky than some of the other options on this list, and it does have soft brushed lining on the inside for some added warmth. The Western-inspired details also set it apart from the more classic styles.

L.L. Bean, the brand known for its rugged outdoor gear, does a cool, modern twist on a classic hunting coat. Working with the originator of waxed cotton, British Millerain, the company designed this option with an innovative fabric that eliminates the typical cleaning fuss that comes with this type of garment.

#6896. A New Marni x Hoka Sneaker Is Here—Here’s What to Know

The Italian fashion house once again brings its unique touch to the athletic brand.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Last year, when Marni and Hoka launched their first collaboration, the two labels came together to bring the world a collection of puffy, chunky, candy-colored takes on the athletic footwear brand’s Bondi sneaker. Naturally, it was a hit among the streetwear set, including the likes of Steph Curry. Of course, a lot can change in the span of a year and a half—and the latest sneaker trends are no exception. In the same way jeans have gone from baggy styles to straight-leg and even skinny jeans, fashion has taken its sneakers from their dad eras to silhouettes that lean a little sleeker. The latest result of these turning tides: The Hoka Clifton Marni.

“Shifting from the maximalist and oversized aesthetic towards a calmer, more essential form made sense with Meryll [Rogge]’s ascension to Marni,” Thomas Cykana, Hoka’s senior director of global collaborations, tells ELLE in an email. “This affords a simple but surprising design that cues premium embellishments, metallic hardware, and unexpected prints over an overtly in-your-face design. This shift enables consumers to both craft their uniform and express their individuality without sacrificing Hoka’s pure performance capabilities.”

Available now, the design is the culmination of form and function. Marni brings its unique design perspective to the technical shoe, not only with the chosen colors—Orange, Black, and White—but also with design-forward touches such as woven heel tabs, windowpane-patterned insoles, and three shoelace options for each colorway.

“Marni has a unique ability to take everyday design cues and make them feel artful and unexpected. That lust for curiosity and imaginative nature is what first drew our team to the label and continues to inspire us,” Cykana shares. “Our debut collection in 2025 showed how much our teams share in creativity and craftsmanship, and that trust led us to our unique spin on the iconic Clifton. The editorial tone they delivered to this project reflects the perfect sensation of everyday and extraordinary at once.”

#6893. The 10 Leather Jackets You (Yes, You) Could Actually Pull Off

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

All you need is the right style, and you’re the coolest guy at the bar.

I bought my first leather jacket at a vintage fair in an old English cathedral. I was 19, and a middle-aged British lady told me I looked good in it. Clearly a sales technique, but it worked, and I shelled out my grocery money for it. I had grand plans of fixing up my dad’s old Honda the following semester and riding around campus in a sick jacket. For various reasons, that didn’t end up happening, and I ended up on a 50cc Yamaha Zuma, a fun little scooter but one where a creaky moto jacket looked more like a comic prop than something out of a Dennis Hopper film. C’est la vie; we all look a little ridiculous in our 20s.

Despite what cinema tells you, you don’t need to ride a motorcycle in order to wear a leather jacket. Or fly a WWII-era fighter plane. Or be Steve McQueen photographed for Esquire. Leather jackets are imbued with the undeniable cool factor of their original functionality—like work jackets, chore coats, and waxed canvas, the modern menswear landscape supports multiple usages. From happy hours to midwestern winters to, yes, actually riding a motorcycle, we’ve rounded up nine Esquire-approved picks to guide your foray into the iconic outerwear. And to come full circle, I did actually test some of these on a motorcycle. More than a decade later, I finally got my license and am fixing up that old Honda; thank God for YouTube.

Leather jackets can be tricky to get right. Too trend focused and it’s a thousand-dollar flash in the pan; too trend agnostic and you get a museum artifact rather than a wearable piece. Ayr nailed the balance on its cleverly named Scrambler jacket, which draws on moto history and sits in the Goldilocks zone between classic and contemporary.

I’ve worn it motorcycling through the Appalachian mountain roads and plan on packing it for an upcoming jaunt to Europe. If you’re looking for a more classic moto style, I also own and highly endorse the elegantly named Baby Grand. The brand (and menswear lead Brice Pattison) has the Midas touch of outerwear; every style is worth its weight in gold.

Sure, there are excellent (and expensive) picks from Loewe, Celine, and Tom Ford with extra-premium materials, brand-specific takes on classic silhouettes, and the extra designer oomph that makes them worth it in some circles. But in this price range, I’m more interested in the experimental and new perspective of a brand like Stòffa, which you can tell is having fun with fabric and silhouettes. This Wave Leather jacket somehow manages to be sleek and drape-y all at once. A daydream of a jacket that’s definitely pricey but somehow still more affordable by half than other designer pieces out there.

Budget leather goods can be problematic. Under a certain price threshold, you’re almost certainly not getting the real thing, in which case you’d be better off going with a disclosed imitation-leather piece from the likes of all-star mall brands like Banana Republic and Abercrombie & Fitch. Or eBay, where a tape measure and a search for Wilson’s Leather or vintage Orvis will unlock a treasure trove of perfectly patinated jackets.

For brand-new on a budget, I recommend the Jacket Maker. Its cut will be a little less forgiving than those of the pricier brands in this list, but if you’re a guy who fits well into off-the-rack suits, you shouldn’t have an issue. Plus, the brand offers a custom, made-to-measure jacket-design tool that has me tempted to re-create McQueen’s from The Great Escape.

This is the one on this list that takes the most bravado. This is the one that upon first wear will get you some looks for your usual drinking buddies. And with good reason—a Schott Perfecto is a loaded garment. Marlon Brando, James Dean, the Ramones, and any cool guy that’s worn one before are not easy acts to follow. But if this is what the heart wants, we stand behind you fully.

The key is in how you wear it. Crucially, you should not “style” it. You should pick it up as much as possible and wear it with as many disparate collections of clothes as you can. For the first few weeks, you might be seen as “The guy trying to wear a leather jacket.” But after a month or two, that will turn into “The leather jacket guy.” Wear it hard, wear it proud, and wear it everywhere.

And one note on the type of leather. If you’re buying new, we think you should go for horsehide. It’s the toughest of the bunch and takes the longest to mold to your body, but that makes it fun.

A shearling jacket is hard to pull off in day-to-day life. Done incorrectly, it can quickly devolve into a kind of cosplay of a WWII fighter pilot. Overland’s Lance jacket retains the genuine sheepskin lining but softens the look with a handsome dark navy colorway paired with a more subtle brown lining, giving you the warmth and badassery without crossing over into costume territory—unless that’s your thing, in which case the brand’s B-3 bomber re-creation is right up your alley. (Though, as always, Todd Snyder’s take is more wearable.)

I want every leather jacket to have an origin story like Taylor Stitch’s, which begins with one of the brand’s designers re-creating a beloved café-racer-style jacket his dad bought in the ’70s and wore to perfection. It turns out the original manufacturer, Golden Bear, was around the corner from TS, and the two brands worked together to reissue the style.

It’s got some modern twists like a Thinsulate lining but retains the design cues that make it functional on a bike and just plain cool on its own. I’ve coveted this jacket for years and recently got my hands on it IRL, and, boy, it does not disappoint. The Whiskey Steerhide colorway looks a little early-aughts coded online, but in person it looks like the magic liquid itself: golden and invigorating. For a non-insulated, more lifestyle-oriented take on the silhouette, I’d point you to Billy Reid’s Blake jacket, which is my dive bar go-to.

Rough out has the wearability of suede, and doesn’t look as turbo as smooth leather. But unlike typical suede, which splits the original hide, rough out retains the whole of the hide. That makes it just as durable and water-resistant as any other leather.

Add that to Billy Reid’s Blake rider shape, and you get a jacket that any guy—literally any guy, we swear—off the street could toss on and feel good in.

Look, the whole oversized vibe is cool and all—but we’re also keenly aware it doesn’t work for everyone. Sure, it brings that chill, relaxed vibe. But sometimes you can just end up looking like a kid wearing their dad’s clothes. If you’re looking for something that hugs the body a bit more, opt for Mili Lotan’s Hammond jacket. It’s got enough slouch you could wear a few layers beneath, but will flatter the body a little more than some of the other options on this list.

I am big on brands that care about details and let the clothes speak for themselves. Octobre Éditions is one such brand. The cut and the cloth set the clothes apart and create longevity outside the trend cycle. When you’ve designed a perfect OCBD, you can never really go out of style. Same too with the brand’s excellent Larren jacket, which I have and is unfortunately currently OOS. Thankfully, the Peylor cuts the same silhouette in a nubby split leather that I’m a little envious of. Get it while the getting’s good.

This is a dream leather jacket—the sort of thing you’d have on a list of “Things I’d Buy If I Had All The Money In The World.” It’s rugged, it’s suave, and damn is it comfy. Just looking at that sumptuous shearling tells you this is a jacket you’d never want to take off, especially on those brutal winter days.

Our editors spend a lot of time assessing what’s in, what’s cool, and what you should care about. It’s literally part of our whole job. Sometimes that means trying things out first-hand, sometimes it’s about thorough research, and sometimes it’s just something you know. Whatever the means, we’ve done the work to figure out what is and isn’t worth your time and money.

#6891. Swimply: Regulatory Affairs Manager

Swimply is the marketplace that lets homeowners rent out their private pools, hot tubs, courts and backyards by the hour. Thousands of hosts across the United States and Canada use Swimply to share space they already own with families and small groups in their community.

Because hourly backyard rentals are new, most local codes were written before they existed. Towns, counties, health departments and homeowners associations regularly try to fit Swimply into rules written for hotels, public pools, home businesses or overnight short-term rentals. We are building a dedicated regulatory function to manage those matters at scale and to move the rules in our favor, state by state.

You will own the day-to-day operation of the program and work directly with our General Counsel, our CEO, our Head of Operations and the outside lobbyists and local counsel we retain.

The Regulatory Affairs Manager runs Swimply's regulatory program end to end. Half of the job is operational: every notice a host or the company receives is logged, answered and tracked to a close. The other half is strategic: finding the bills, partners and officials that let us fix the rules for a whole state at once instead of town by town.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.

#6888. Jensen Huang’s Shoes Were the Tell

I like men’s shoes and I’ve sold men’s shoes. A man’s footwear reveals facets of his public persona—and his private desires.

I spent much of Thursday staring at photographs of Jensen Huang’s shoes arranged in one soldierly line longer in both directions than the frame could hold. These dozen pairs—each different, all dazzling—were but a glimpse of his footwear armies. Jensen’s Hermès slip-ons and dozens of black leather jackets are not only his fashion signatures; they’re also proof of his decision to not waste precious energy deciding what clothes to wear each morning.

This is a philosophy I share with Jensen, and my version saves even more time and energy: Don’t change clothes. But I like men’s shoes, I’ve sold men’s shoes, and I believe that a man’s shoes reveal facets of his public persona and private desires.

In Jensen’s case, there’s no mystery. He’s a brilliant guy who was once a small, smart immigrant kid bullied and beaten at a Kentucky Baptist reform school that his uncle mistook for a boarding school. Now he’s 63, a five-foot-six AI titan standing in adorable—size 9 is my guess—multi-thousand-dollar slip-ons atop his $140 billion, with his dick out and dripping all over his kicks du jour.

And we are left to sort the tragedy, seek catharsis, and remind ourselves again—and again—that Jensen Huang’s downfall awaits any man who ignores the fundamental law of our own animal nature: Ven der putz shteht, der sechel licht in drert: When the penis stands, the brain goes to hell.

I make no moral judgment here; we have no basis or right to do so. The college sweetheart he married 41 years ago, along with their two grown children, are the only souls with standing in this case. But I think it’s only fair, in light of current reporting on Jensen’s three concurrent affairs—that’s two side pieces plus Princess Honey-Pot plus a wife—to reconsider that vast battalion of decision-saving slip-ons and what it says.

The shoes reveal a man with smallish feet whose penis was too busy making decisions to consider laces, love, or the welfare of his family and business. And there aren’t enough shoes, dollars, or women in the world to fill the hole in Jensen Huang.

The Latin word ‘invidia’ means envy. This, given Jensen’s shoes, is not coincidence or irony; it’s a weakling’s shamed confession. Hermes needs to cobble one last pair for Jensen Huang, long, bright red, and floppy.

#6886. The Secret to Kendall Jenner’s ‘Me Mode’

The model and founder shares her favorite ways to unwind and how the Therabody TheraFace Mask Glo fits into her nightly routine.

For Kendall Jenner, “Me Mode” is a time to unplug from the daily demands of work and social obligations and simply be still. “I have a really busy mind, so switching off has never come naturally to me,” says the model and entrepreneur. “I’ve had to build little rituals that help me slow down.”

One of those rituals involves Therabody’s TheraFace Mask Glo, an LED face mask that bridges skincare with wellness. Delivering full LED coverage across the entire face, including the lips and under eyes, the cordless device features 504 red, infrared, and blue LEDs, plus a relaxing scalp massage to help relieve tension. Best of all, treatment time is only 12 minutes, giving Jenner a quick, easy, and much-needed pause from her busy schedule.

“After a long day, it’s sweatpants, my dogs, and zero plans,” Jenner says. “Putting on the Mask Glo has kind of become my signal that the day is over. For those 12 minutes, my brain finally goes quiet.”

One thing Jenner loves about the TheraFace Mask Glo is how effortlessly it fits into her routine. “It’s hands-free, so I just put it on and do whatever I feel like that night,” Jenner says. “Sometimes that’s music or journaling, and a lot of the time it’s doing absolutely nothing. The scalp massage is my favorite part. It feels less like a skincare step and more like actually relaxing.”

It offers legitimate skincare benefits, too. Backed by the largest clinical study of any LED mask on the market, the TheraFace Mask Glo was shown to reduce under-eye wrinkles and fine lines, enhance radiance, and lift sagging skin in as little as eight weeks.

The TheraFace Mask Glo offers three LED settings to target different skin concerns. Red light helps improve the appearance of fine lines, wrinkles, and dark spots, while red and infrared light work together to boost collagen production. Blue light targets acne-causing bacteria, helping prevent future breakouts.

The key is consistent use, which Jenner has found easy to maintain. “If a routine is too complicated, I’m not going to stick with it, and I’ve learned that consistency matters more than anything,” she says. “With the Mask Glo, I pick red, red and infrared, or blue light depending on how my skin is acting, and that’s it. It’s easy enough that I actually use it, which is half the battle for me. It comes with me when I travel now, too.”

The LED lights are placed across the entire face, even covering overlooked areas like the mouth and eyes. The preset treatment runs for 12 minutes, cycling through four minutes of each light setting. Four-minute individual treatments are also available.

Combining advanced skincare and relaxation into one simple step, the TheraFace Mask Glo is ideal for anyone who wants to level up their nighttime routine with minimal effort. As Jenner has learned over the years, less is more when it comes to beauty and wellness.

“I’ve been sitting in makeup chairs since I was 14, so I went through a phase of trying everything,” she says. “Now I do a lot less, and I’m more intentional about what I put on my skin. I try to give it a break when I can, and I’ll use the Mask Glo before glam on shoot days.”

Jenner’s nighttime routine is just one aspect in living a more holistic lifestyle. “Wellness for me is mostly about balance now: being at the barn with my horses, getting outside, and protecting my nights,” she says. “My friends make fun of me for going to bed so early, but I’m not changing.”

#6883. The 6 Skincare Lessons Every Middle-Aged Man Should Know

Esquire asked the glowiest people we know for their suggestions—and rounded up some top-notch product recommendations, too.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

In 2026, young men are generally on top of their skincare. But in the before times, a splash of cold water to the face was the extent of most morning regimens. That’s probably why so many middle-aged guys find themselves with dried-out, sun-damaged skin that isn’t quite where they want it to be. The good news is that it’s never too late to pick up good habits.

With that in mind, we asked six of the glowiest people we know to share the products they reach for every day—and the lessons they’ve learned along the way.

Caroline Hirons, aesthetician and writer: "Men’s skin is slightly thicker, and a high majority of men shave daily, which can cause its own set of problems if not done properly. Using the right kit and products is important: shaving oils, facial shaving products, post-shave moisturizers … A lot of men with a heavier beard would also benefit from using a daily acid exfoliator to prevent ingrown hairs.”

“Skin Rules” by Caroline Hirons is out now

Saul Nash, menswear designer: "“Korean skincare is everything. I use Reedle Shot 100 masks—I love them and they leave my skin glowing. I do think it’s important, though, not to trouble your skin too much—I have combination [different conditions across the face] to oily skin, so I try not to touch my face a lot or put too many products on it.”

Harris Reed, designer: “It’s all about eye cream and a silk eye mask—these are non-negotiables.”

Dr Richard Devine, aesthetic doctor and founder of Devine Clinic: “A product that’s working shouldn’t leave your skin feeling persistently red, sore, itchy, or inflamed. Mild adjustment periods can occur with certain active ingredients, but ongoing discomfort is usually a sign that the skin barrier is becoming irritated rather than improved. If redness, burning, or sensitivity continues beyond the first few uses, it’s often worth reducing frequency or reassessing whether the product is appropriate for your skin.”

Ziad Halub, co-owner of Iranian-Iraqi café bistro Logma in Hackney: “After a hot and sticky day, your skin needs help. Sweat, dead skin, SPF, pollution, and general city grime can build into a layer of filth. A good foaming cleanser, one that doesn’t strip the skin, is essential. You want fresh, not squeaky. I’m currently loving African Botanics’ Baobab Clay Oxygenating Cleanser.”

Marc Terlet, co-founder of French grooming brand Horace: “At a minimum, an effective skincare routine involves washing your face, applying moisturizer, and then sunscreen. Make things easier with an SPF moisturizer but remember to reapply every couple of hours. The sun is responsible for 80 per cent of your skin aging. Imagine how young you would look if you’d used sunscreen every day since you were 20?”
